2 
I. 
INTRODUCTION AND OVERVIEW 
1 
Q. 
Would you please state your name and business address? 
2 
A. 
My name is Danielle M. Panko, and my business address is 99 Washington 
3 
Avenue, Suite 640, Albany, NY 12231.  
4 
 
5 
Q. 
By whom are you employed, in what capacity, and what are your professional 
6 
backgrounds and qualifications? 
7 
A. 
I currently hold the position of a Utility Analyst with the Utility Intervention Unit (UIU) 
8 
of the New York State Department of State’s Division of Consumer Protection.  I 
9 
received a Bachelor of Science in Mathematics from the State University of New 
10 
York at New Paltz in 2001 and a Master of Science in Electrical Engineering from 
11 
the State University of New York at New Paltz in 2008.   
12 
From 2000 to 2001, I served as an intern with Central Hudson Gas and Electric 
13 
Corporation (Central Hudson) located in Poughkeepsie, New York, in the Accounts 
14 
Service Department and subsequently in the Electrical Engineering Department.  
15 
From 2004 to 2007, I worked as a reliability engineer for Philips Semiconductors.  
16 
From 2007 to 2012, I worked for Consolidated Edison Company of New York, Inc. 
17 
(Con Edison) in the Rate Engineering Department as an Analyst, and later as a 
18 
Senior Analyst, in the Gas Rate Design Section.  I joined the UIU in 2012.  My 
19 
primary responsibilities include assisting with UIU's participation in Public Service 
20 
Commission (PSC or Commission) proceedings, researching utility policy and 
21 
regulatory related issues, and representing UIU during various utility-related 
22 
1346


--- Page 19 ---
CASE 19-G-0309 and 19-G-0310  
 
             
 DIRECT TESTIMONY OF  
                                                                                      DANIELLE PANKO 
3 
meetings and rate case negotiations.  I have worked on a myriad of New York 
1 
utility cases in addition to other rate and policy proceedings.  
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Have you previously testified before the Commission? 
4 
A. 
Yes.  I previously submitted testimony in Cases 13-E-0030 and 13-G-0031 (Con 
5 
Edison), 14-E-0318 and 14-G-0319 (Central Hudson), 14-E-0493 and 14-G-0494 
6 
(Orange and Rockland Utilities, Inc. (Orange and Rockland)), 15-E-0283, 15-G-
7 
0284, 15-E-0285 and 15-G-0286 (New York State Electric and Gas Corporation 
8 
(NYSEG) and Rochester Gas and Electric (RG&E)), 16-G-0058 and 16-G-0059 
9 
(The Brooklyn Union Gas Company d/b/a National Grid NY (KEDNY) and KeySpan 
10 
Gas East Corp. d/b/a National Grid (KEDLI)), 16-G-0060 and 16-G-0061 (Con 
11 
Edison), 16-G-0257 (National Fuel Gas Company (National Fuel)), 17-E-0238 and 
12 
17-G-0239 (Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation d/b/a National Grid (National 
13 
Grid)), 17-E-0459 and 17-G-0460 (Central Hudson), and 18-E-0067 and 18-G-
14 
0068 (Orange and Rockland). 
15 
 
16 
Q. 
What is the scope of this testimony?  
17 
A. 
I will primarily comment on the Embedded Cost of Service (ECOS) studies, 
18 
revenue allocation, and rate design proposed by KEDNY and KEDLI (together, the 
19 
Companies), as set forth in the Direct Testimony of the Rate Design Panel 
20 
(KEDNY) and the Direct Testimony of the Rate Design Panel (KEDLI).  The 
21 
absence of discussion of other topics in this testimony should not be construed as 
22 
support for, or opposition to, the Companies’ positions. 
23 
1347


--- Page 20 ---
CASE 19-G-0309 and 19-G-0310  
 
             
 DIRECT TESTIMONY OF  
                                                                                      DANIELLE PANKO 
4 
Q. 
How is your testimony organized?   
1 
A. 
This testimony has six sections.  The first section is an introduction to the 
2 
forthcoming testimony.  Second, I summarize my recommendations.  Third, I 
3 
provide the background of this current proceeding, including a discussion of the 
4 
Companies’ previous rate cases.  Fourth, I discuss issues with the Companies’ 
5 
gas cost of service studies.  Fifth, I discuss revenue allocation issues.  Finally, in 
6 
the sixth section, I discuss a few gas rate design issues.   
7 
 
8 
Q. 
Have you prepared any exhibits to be filed with your testimony?  
9 
A. 
Yes, Exhibit___(DP-1) and Exhibit___(DP-2) accompanies my testimony.  
10 
Exhibit___(DP-1) contains my proposed revenue allocation process for both 
11 
KEDNY and KEDLI.  Exhibit___(DP-2) contains the Companies’ responses to 
12 
Information Requests (IRs) that I relied upon in preparing this testimony.   
13 
 
14 
II. 
SUMMARY OF RECOMMENDATIONS 
15 
Q. 
Please briefly summarize your recommendations. 
16 
A. 
My recommendations are as follows: 
17 
 
18 
A. COST OF SERVICE 
19 
First, I recommend the Companies utilize the results of both pro-forma gas ECOS 
20 
studies filed in each of these cases as a guide to understanding the cost of 
21 
providing service to each of the individual service classifications (SC).  Second, in 
22 
the next set of rate filings, I recommend the Companies again provide the results 
23 
1348


--- Page 21 ---
CASE 19-G-0309 and 19-G-0310  
 
             
 DIRECT TESTIMONY OF  
                                                                                      DANIELLE PANKO 
5 
of multiple gas ECOS studies for use as a guide to allocate costs.  Specifically, the 
1 
Companies should submit ECOS studies in which the distribution costs are 
2 
classified 100 percent (%) as demand-related, as they did in these rate cases. This 
3 
proposal is consistent with the ECOS models presented in some other New York 
4 
utility rate cases and/or rate plans that have previously been approved by the 
5 
Commission.  Third, no less than two months prior to the intervenor direct 
6 
testimony deadline, each of the Companies should invite all parties in its next rate 
7 
case to attend a meeting (outside of its technical conference), at which the 
8 
Companies would present an overview of the detailed data set forth in its filed gas 
9 
cost of service studies, proposed revenue allocation process, and proposed rate 
10 
design (similar to the process adopted in the Commission’s Orders in Case 15-E-
11 
0050 et al.1 and 18-E-0067 et al.2). 
12 
 
13 
B.  
 REVENUE ALLOCATION 
14 
I recommend the revenue allocation process be based on the output, or results, of 
15 
the two types of ECOS studies filed in each of the utilities’ rate cases.  The 
16 
Companies filed one pro-forma ECOS model that utilizes a minimum system 
17 
methodology to classify distribution main costs as both customer-related and 
18 
demand-related.  The Companies’ second pro-forma ECOS model is based on the 
19 
 
1  See Cases 15-E-0050 et al., Order Adopting Terms of Joint Proposal to Extend Electric Rate Plan at 
Appendix A p. 20 (filed June 19, 2015). 
2  See Cases 18-E-0067 et al., Order Adopting Terms of Joint Proposal and Establishing Electric and Gas 
Rate Plans Attachment A November 9, 2018 Joint Proposal (issued March 14, 2019) (gas ECOS study 
discussed on pp. 43-44). 
1349


--- Page 22 ---
CASE 19-G-0309 and 19-G-0310  
 
             
 DIRECT TESTIMONY OF  
                                                                                      DANIELLE PANKO 
6 
classification of distribution main costs as 100 % demand-related.  While I do not 
1 
necessarily agree with the minimum system method (which is the only type of 
2 
ECOS study the Companies used for revenue allocation purposes in these cases), 
3 
collectively the two ECOS models filed in these cases provide a range of possible 
4 
outcomes that are consistent with past methodologies approved by the 
5 
Commission in utility rate cases for revenue allocation purposes.  
6 
Furthermore, to avoid placing an excessive burden on any particular SC 
7 
and to ensure that all SCs help to share some of the burden of the rate increase, I 
8 
propose the Commission set boundaries around the amount of the base delivery 
9 
revenue change applied to each SC during the revenue allocation process.  For 
10 
example, a potential boundary structure may include the following guidelines: no 
11 
SC should receive a revenue change more or less than 25% to 50% of overall 
12 
revenue increase and no SC receives a revenue decrease.  
13 
For illustrative purposes, Exhibit___(DP-1) shows the effects of applying 
14 
boundaries of 0.75 and 1.25 (KEDNY) and 0.5 and 1.5 (KEDLI) times the system 
15 
average percentage increase to the revenue allocation process in these rate 
16 
cases.  However, I recognize that somewhat different boundaries might be 
17 
appropriate depending on the final revenue requirements in these cases.  Thus, I 
18 
recommend the Commission set specific boundaries around the amount of base 
19 
delivery revenue change applied to individual SCs to reflect the magnitude of the 
20 
delivery revenue increase that is ultimately adopted by the Commission in these 
21 
proceedings. 
22 
1350


--- Page 23 ---
CASE 19-G-0309 and 19-G-0310  
 
             
 DIRECT TESTIMONY OF  
                                                                                      DANIELLE PANKO 
7 
For illustrative purposes, Exhibit___(DP-1) shows the results of utilizing the 
1 
Companies’ proposed tolerance band of plus or minus (+/-) 25% around the 
2 
system average Rate of Return (ROR) to determine if SCs are under or 
3 
overearning (or within the tolerance band) according to the results in the 
4 
Companies’ two filed ECOS models.  I propose if the results of both ECOS models 
5 
indicate the ROR for a SC is within the tolerance band (for example, within +/- 25% 
6 
of the average ROR), then the SC should receive close to the system average 
7 
delivery revenue increase over present rates.  If both ECOS models confirm a SC 
8 
is earning below the tolerance band, the class should receive an increase that is 
9 
higher than average (but no more than the maximum increase established in the 
10 
boundaries described above).  If both ECOS models confirm a class is earning 
11 
above the tolerance band, the class should receive an increase that is lower than 
12 
average (but not less than the minimum increase).  If the two ECOS models 
13 
provide contradictory ROR results (e.g., the one study shows the class earning 
14 
below the tolerance band and the other study shows the class earning above the 
15 
tolerance band), the rate class should receive a delivery revenue increase that is 
16 
close to the system average increase. Additionally, while Exhibit___(DP-1) 
17 
includes temperature controlled (TC) and interruptible (IT) customers in this 
18 
revenue allocation process for illustrative purposes, I note that some utilities 
19 
exclude TC/IT customers from ECOS studies3 and instead adopt market-based 
20 
 
3  See e.g., Case 18-G-0068, Direct Testimony of Gas Rate Panel pp. 8-9 (filed January 26, 2018) (stating 
that the ECOS study analyzes Orange and Rockland’s two firm classes and gas revenues from 
interruptible service are excluded from the ECOS study).

rates for those customers.  An alternative approach to revenue allocation for those 
1 
classes may be warranted depending on market conditions, or if those customers 
2 
are not included in the ECOS studies.

4 
C.  
RATE DESIGN 
5 
My testimony provides general recommendations for rate design that can be 
6 
further explored, as appropriate, depending on the Companies’ final revenue 
7 
requirements.  The starting point for my recommended rate design approach is my 
8 
proposed revenue allocation process, which I used to assign costs to individual 
9 
service classes (e.g., setting a revenue target for each SC).  However, I observe 
10 
there are a variety of factors that may change the revenue target allocated to each 
11 
SC, such as a change in the Companies’ revenue requirements that requires more 
12 
or less revenues to be collected from all SCs.  The revenue target allocated to 
13 
each SC together with the fixed monthly customer charge and portion of the 
14 
revenues that flow through each of the volumetric rates (e.g., increasing the ratio 
15 
of tail-block to mid-block) can potentially have an impact on the optimal rate design 
16 
for each SC.  As such, my rate design recommendations are explanatory at this 
17 
time, since my recommendation will be dependent upon the final revenue 
18 
requirements, which will influence the magnitude of any potential changes to the 
19 
customer charge and volumetric rates. 
 
20 
1352


--- Page 25 ---
CASE 19-G-0309 and 19-G-0310  
 
             
 DIRECT TESTIMONY OF  
                                                                                      DANIELLE PANKO 
9 
Residential Non-Heating – SC 1A  
1 
I recommend gradually moving away from the existing, steeply declining volumetric 
2 
block rate design.  The movement must be gradual to avoid rate shock to high 
3 
usage customers.  My recommendation for the amount of moderate change to be 
4 
implemented will vary depending on several factors, such as if the Companies’ 
5 
residential SC 1A class revenues are reduced as per my revenue allocation 
6 
proposals and/or if the Companies’ overall revenue requirements are reduced.  For 
7 
the higher users (e.g., customers who use greater than 500 therms per month as 
8 
typical customers use 10-21 therms), the Companies should check to make sure 
9 
these customers are in fact non-heating customers, and move them to the correct 
10 
class, where applicable.   
11 
 
12 
Residential Heating – SC 1B  
13 
The Companies’ residential SC 1B heating rates should be a designed to more 
14 
effectively encourage energy efficiency improvements.  As such, the SC 1B 
15 
heating rates should, at a minimum, be gradually moved away from a declining 
16 
volumetric rate toward a flat rate.  This recommendation contrasts with the 
17 
Companies’ proposal to continue a declining rate structure for SC 1B with a similar 
18 
tail-block rate (Block 3) to mid-block rate (Block 2) ratio, because that approach 
19 
makes no movement toward encouraging greater energy efficiency.  The 
20 
magnitude of my proposed change will be dependent upon the final revenue 
21 
requirements.

 DIRECT TESTIMONY OF  
                                                                                      DANIELLE PANKO 
18 
IV. 
COST OF SERVICE 
1 
A. 
Background 
2 
Q. 
Please provide a few brief introductory comments concerning ECOS studies. 
3 
A. 
An ECOS study is often used as a tool in utility rate proceedings to apportion rate 
4 
base and operating expenses among the various customer classes.  This data is 
5 
used in conjunction with existing revenues to estimate the overall system ROR and 
6 
the ROR earned from each service classification as defined in the study.  
7 
Sometimes, the focus is on historical revenues; other times forecasted revenue 
8 
increases for the Rate Year (pro-forma) are used to estimate the overall system 
9 
ROR (similar to what the Companies proposed in these cases).  Regardless of the 
10 
specific approach that is used, the analyst must exercise discretion and judgment 
11 
in apportioning costs.  Some of these judgments have been the subject of much 
12 
debate and controversy throughout the last 40 years or more.20 
13 
The end result of an ECOS study is to estimate the overall system ROR and 
14 
individual service classification RORs.  The ECOS study results are particularly 
15 
significant because of their potential impact on revenue allocation – how the rate 
16 
 
20  For example, the National Association of Regulatory Utility Commissioners Gas Distribution Rate 
Design Manual, published in June 1989 (NARUC Manual) discusses the general judgements that can 
be used in an ECOS model. See e.g., National Association of Regulatory Utility Commissioners Gas 
Distribution Rate Design Manual (published June 1989) p. 20 (stating “[t]here is no scientifically correct 
method of making necessary allocations. A certain amount of judgement must be used in any cost of 
service study. Consequently, cost allocation studies should only be utilized as a general guide or as a 
starting point for rate design.”) One particularly controversial judgement has been the decision 
regarding the assignment of customer costs, particularly whether costs associated with the distribution 
system should be included as customer costs; the NARUC Manual recognizes the 100% demand 
approach. (id. at p. 22-23) (noting “[t]he contra argument to the inclusion of certain distribution costs is 
that mains and services are installed to serve demands of the consumers and should be allocated to 
that function”).   
1362


--- Page 35 ---
CASE 19-G-0309 and 19-G-0310  
 
             
 DIRECT TESTIMONY OF  
                                                                                      DANIELLE PANKO 
19 
increases are assigned to the various customer classes.  The ECOS results, or 
1 
output, are sometimes also used to support aspects of the rate design – particularly 
2 
the monthly customer charge – within individual SCs.   
3 
The remainder of this section of my testimony will primarily focus on the 
4 
Companies’ decision to use a minimum system approach in an ECOS study to 
5 
classify some distribution main costs as customer-related, instead of classifying 
6 
these costs as 100% demand-related.   
7 
 
8 
Q. 
You mentioned that some of the judgments used within cost of service studies 
9 
have been controversial.  Can you elaborate on this point with some examples? 
10 
A. 
Yes.  Some gas and electric utilities analyze a portion of their distribution costs 
11 
using some variation of a minimum system or a statistically-based zero-intercept 
12 
method – thereby classifying a portion of these distribution costs as customer 
13 
related.  Other utilities choose not to prepare this type of analysis – they do not 
14 
classify or allocate any of the distribution costs based on the number of customers. 
15 
Even utilities that use a minimum system or zero-intercept approach may not fully 
16 
implement this controversial approach when developing their revenue allocation 
17 
and rate design proposals.21  In recent years, I have seen how controversial the 
18 
judgments associated with the classification of costs as “customer related” can be 
19 
in the context of various New York utility rate cases,22 as well as the Value of 
20 
 
21  For example, see Companies’ proposed revenue allocation process in their 2016 rate cases. See Cases 
16-G-0058 and 16-G-0059, Testimony and Exhibits of Rate Design Panel (Book 16 for both KEDLI and 
KEDNY) pp. 23-24 (filed January 29, 2016). 
22  See e.g., Cases 16-E-0060 et al, Order Approving Electric and Gas Rate Plans pp. 44-45 (issued 
January 25, 2017) (noting “there can be reasonable disagreement over the preferred methods for

8 
Q. 
Why does it matter what judgments and datasets are used in a utility cost of service 
9 
study? 
10 
A. 
These judgments and datasets can impact the final RORs for individual service 
11 
classifications.  Even though these RORs are the result of controversial judgments, 
12 
they largely determine whether classes are deemed to be under-earning or over-
13 
earning during the revenue allocation process. In turn, this determines how large 
14 
a rate increase will be assigned to each of the service classifications.  
15 
Q. 
Should the Companies’ preferred ECOS results be used as the sole basis to 
16 
allocate the revenue requirement for each utility across service classifications?  
17 
A. 
No.  Based on my experience reviewing ECOS models and revenue allocation 
18 
proposals in various New York utility rate cases over the past few years, I believe 
19 
it is appropriate to start incorporating several ECOS models as a guide to allocate 
20 
costs.  This provides more insight into the impact of key judgments, it will help the 
21 
 
such as: (1) cost of service, (2) revenue allocation, (3) authorized deferrals, (4) additional flex rates, 
and (5) hourly pricing).

Commission make better, more informed decisions based on the results, and it will 
1 
ameliorate, to some degree, the controversy that surrounds judgments concerning 
2 
the appropriate definition and interpretation of so-called “customer-related” costs.

Q. 
Has the minimum system approach been universally accepted by either utilities or 
5 
regulators? 
6 
A. 
No.  This approach is not universally used, and where it has been used it has often 
7 
been very controversial.  Even when it has been used, it has not necessarily been 
8 
fully relied upon (see for example, the KEDNY and KEDLI 2016 rate cases 
9 
previously discussed in this testimony, in which the final revenue allocation was 
10 
not entirely driven by the ECOS results).   
11

13 
to classify distribution mains as partly customer-related? 
14 
A. 
No.  I am aware of some Pennsylvania gas utility proceedings in which Mr. Gorman 
15 
allocated none of the distribution main costs as customer-related; instead these 
16 
costs were classified as demand-related and the costs were allocated using the 
17 
average and excess method.40

Recommendations

Q. 
What do you recommend with respect to using ECOS results for revenue allocation 
14 
purposes in these cases? 
15

16

17 
ECOS studies proposed by the Companies, which use the minimum system 
18 
approach.  While I do not necessarily agree with the ECOS model that classifies 
19 
distribution main costs as partly customer-related, I recognize the Companies 
20 
appear to prefer this approach.  The Commission has sometimes accepted ECOS 
21 
results using a minimum system approach, just as it has sometimes accepted 
22 
results in which distribution mains were classified as 100% demand-related.  In the

14

15

18 
in the next rate cases to attend a meeting where the parties and their experts can 
19 
review the detailed data set forth in its cost of service studies, the proposed

21

22 
presentation should mirror the requirements approved by the Commission in the

Q.  
Do you have any concerns with the use of ECOS studies to allocate costs to TC 
4 
and IT customers? 
5 
A.  
Yes. I have concerns regarding the Companies’ proposal to use ECOS results to 
6

7 
do not use an ECOS study to design their interruptible rates.42  Further, excluding 
8 
these customers from the ECOS study gives the Companies more freedom to 
9 
design IT/TC rates.  However, if the Companies’ proposed approach to cost-based 
10 
IT/TC customer rates is accepted and these classes continue to be a part of ECOS 
11 
studies, then TC/IT classes should be subject to the same boundary and rules 
12 
applied to other SCs.

 DIRECT TESTIMONY OF  
                                                                                      DANIELLE PANKO 
31 
IV. 
REVENUE ALLOCATION 
1 
A. 
Background 
2 
Q. 
Can you briefly explain the revenue allocation process in a typical utility rate case? 
3 
A. 
Yes.  The results of one or more ECOS studies guide decisions regarding how 
4 
proposed rate changes should be allocated across various service classifications.  
5 
The specific process can vary, but ECOS results are generally examined to see 
6 
whether certain classes have been identified as having a high ROR (paying more 
7 
than the costs they have been allocated – sometimes referred to as “overearning”).  
8 
If a high ROR is identified, an effort is typically made to assign a somewhat smaller 
9 
share of the rate increase or revenue requirement to that service classification, 
10 
thereby moving in the direction of greater uniformity in the service classification 
11 
RORs.  Similarly, if certain classes are identified as having a low ROR (paying less 
12 
than the costs that have been allocated to them, which is sometimes described as 
13 
“underearning”), a somewhat greater than average share of the rate increase or 
14 
revenue burden may be assigned to that class during the revenue allocation step. 
15 
In carrying out the revenue allocation process, tolerance bands are often 
16 
used around the system average ROR to decide whether the service classification 
17 
ROR deviates far enough from the average ROR to merit special treatment.  The 
18 
width of the tolerance band (e.g., +-10%, 20%, 30%, etc.) can vary widely, but the 
19 
effect is generally the same – to ameliorate disputes concerning the ECOS results, 
20 
1375


--- Page 48 ---
CASE 19-G-0309 and 19-G-0310  
 
             
 DIRECT TESTIMONY OF  
                                                                                      DANIELLE PANKO 
32 
since service classifications that fall within the tolerance band generally receive 
1 
the same percentage change.43  
2 
An analyst must exercise judgment when determining the size of the 
3 
tolerance band, and this is sometimes a topic of dispute.  The tolerance band can 
4 
be important because it helps determine which service classifications will receive 
5 
revenue changes that are less than, or more than, the other service classifications.  
6 
Finally, some restrictions are typically imposed on how much any service 
7 
classification will deviate from the average (e.g., no class is given a decrease, no 
8 
class is given more than 150% of the average increase). 
9 
The typical process just described is not the only method that has been 
10 
used in New York. Over the years in various proceedings, the Commission has 
11 
sometimes approved other revenue allocation methods.  For example, in the last 
12 
KEDNY and KEDLI gas rate cases (16-G-0058 and 16-G-0059) the Commission 
13 
approved system average increases for most of the Companies’ service 
14 
classifications without regard for the ECOS results.  In those cases, the 
15 
Commission stated in the Order Adopting Terms of the Joint Proposal and 
16 
Establishing Gas Rate Plans on December 16, 2016: 
17 
Pursuant to JP Sections IV.3.2 and V.3.2, the revenue 
18 
increases in each of the rate years would be allocated 
19 
to all firm service classes [service classifications] on an 
20 
equal percentage basis to current delivery revenues, 
21 
 
43  I note that note that Con Edison and Orange and Rockland’s revenue allocation process is unique from 
other New York utilities since these utilities have in past testimony proposed to apply 1/3 of the adjusted 
surplus and deficient costs “in a revenue neutral manner prior to applying the revenue increases.”  See 
e.g., Case 19-G-0061, Direct Testimony of Gas Rates Panel pp. 33-34 (filed January 29, 2019). See 
also, Case 18-G-0068, Gas Rates Panel pp. 23-24 (January 26, 2018).  
1376


--- Page 49 ---
CASE 19-G-0309 and 19-G-0310  
 
             
 DIRECT TESTIMONY OF  
                                                                                      DANIELLE PANKO 
33 
with limited exceptions…. Revenue increases for Rate 
1 
Years 2 and 3 will be similarly allocated. (p. 35). 
2 
 
3 
And: 
4 
 
5 
We agree that given the sizeable revenue increases for 
6 
KEDNY and KEDLI, the equal percentage allocation of 
7 
the revenue increases to all firm service classifications 
8 
would be appropriate to minimize rate shock to the 
9 
extent practicable. (p. 38).  
10 
 
11 
Q. 
Please explain how the Companies propose to distribute the 20.3% increase for 
12 
KEDNY over pro-forma base delivery revenues, and the smaller revenue increase 
13 
for KEDLI (7.8% increase over pro-forma base delivery revenues) among the 
14 
various customer service classifications? 
15 
A. 
The Companies started with the results of its chosen pro-forma gas ECOS Study 
16 
(which classifies distribution mains as partly customer-related using a minimum 
17 
system methodology44), using the following steps: (1) applying a +-25% tolerance 
18 
band around the system average ROR  (service classifications above or below the 
19 
+-25% tolerance band were considered surplus or deficient, respectively); (2) 
20 
assigning revenue changes based on the tolerance band results (generally, 
21 
classes within the tolerance band receive an average increase, classes over the 
22 
band receive smaller increases or a decrease, and classes under the tolerance 
23 
band receive larger increases).45  The tables below, from the Companies technical 
24

customer/demand-related (KEDNY) and 46.17%/53.83% (KEDLI) customer/demand-related. 
45  See Cases 19-G-0309 and 19-G-0310, KEDNY and KEDLI Direct Testimony of the Rate Design Panel 
pp. 26-27 (filed April 30, 2019).

B. 
Recommendations 
1 
Q. 
What is your response to the Companies’ proposed increases in the customer 
2 
charges and volumetric block rate changes for residential accounts? 
3 
A. 
The starting point for my recommended rate design is my proposed revenue 
4 
allocation, which I used to assign costs to individual service classes. There are a 
5 
few variables that impact rates such as: (1) revenue requirement, (2) revenue 
6 
allocation, (3) customer charges, and (4) the portion of the revenues that flow 
7 
through each of the volumetric rates (e.g., increasing the ratio of tail-block to mid-
8 
block).  This discussion is explanatory in nature, since UIU’s recommendation will 
9 
be dependent upon the final revenue requirement for each utility, which will 
10 
influence the magnitude of any potential changes to the customer charge and 
11 
volumetric rates. 
12 
 
13 
Residential Non-Heating – SC 1A  
14 
 
The Companies’ Residential Non-Heating SC 1A classes are very sensitive to any 
15 
rate adjustments.  I recommend moving away from the existing, steeply declining 
16 
rate design.  However, the movement must be gradual.  A moderate change is 
17 
needed to not financially overburden high usage customers (including some low-
18 
income customers). However, if the Companies’ residential SC 1A class revenue 
19 
responsibilities are reduced as per my revenue allocation proposals and/or if the 
20 
overall revenue requirements are reduced in general, then there should be greater 
21 
movement toward flat rates (especially for KEDLI since KEDLI SC 1A has a lower 
22 
1386


--- Page 59 ---
CASE 19-G-0309 and 19-G-0310  
 
             
 DIRECT TESTIMONY OF  
                                                                                      DANIELLE PANKO 
43 
tail-block to mid-block ratio than KEDNY as well as a lower tail-block rate).  For the 
1 
higher users (e.g., customers who use greater than 500 therms per month while 
2 
typical customers use 10-21 therms), the Companies should also check to make 
3 
sure these customers are in fact non-heating customers, and move them to the 
4 
correct class, where applicable.   
5 
 
6 
Residential Heating – SC 1B  
7 
The Companies’ residential SC 1B heating rates should be a designed to more 
8 
effectively encourage energy efficiency improvements.  As such, the SC 1B 
9 
heating rates should, at a minimum, be gradually moved away from a declining 
10 
volumetric rate toward a flat rate.   This recommendation contrasts with the 
11 
Companies’ proposal to continue a declining rate structure for SC 1B with a similar 
12 
tail-block rate (Block 3) to mid-block rate (Block 2) ratio, because that approach 
13 
makes no movement toward encouraging greater energy efficiency.  The 
14 
Companies’ SC 1B tail-block rates are lower than the Companies’ SC 1A tail-block 
15 
rates, but in both cases careful consideration must be made on how far the tail-
16 
block rates can be increased before unacceptably severe bill impacts affect the 
17 
high users in these classes.  As such, the magnitude of my proposed change will 
18 
be dependent upon the final revenue requirements.

Q.

9 
design? 
10 
A. 
Yes.  I recommend the Companies’ continue50 to provide an increased level of 
11 
targeted assistance to residential customers with the highest monthly gas usage 
12 
(including low income customers) to ensure they understand how they can reduce 
13 
their usage.  To achieve the State's goals for energy efficiency, these customers 
14

15

17

The primary objective was to consolidate systems

18 
Customer Service Quality Program 
19 
Q. 
What is the purpose of a Customer Service 
20 
Quality Program (CSQP)? 
21 
A. 
CSQPs help to align shareholder and customer 
22 
interests by providing earnings consequences 
23 
related to the quality of service provided by a 
24 
1461


--- Page 134 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SCSP 
 
 
23 
 
utility to its customers.  Currently, these 
1 
programs are in effect for most major, investor-
2 
owned energy utilities in New York that are 
3 
regulated by the Commission.  The metrics that 
4 
make up the CSQP link earnings directly to a 
5 
utility’s performance on specific measures of 
6 
customer service. 
7 
Q. 
Why are CSQPs imposed by the Commission? 
8 
A. 
As monopoly providers of utility delivery 
9 
service, utilities do not have a profit-based 
10 
incentive to provide acceptable levels of 
11 
customer service as customers cannot select 
12 
another utility based on the quality of service 
13 
provided, however, obtaining quality service is 
14 
extremely important to customers and is in the 
15 
public interest.  A CSQP is needed to establish 
16 
an incentive for each Company to provide 
17 
satisfactory levels of customer service 
18 
performance. 
19 
Q. 
Please describe how Staff reviews the Companies’ 
20 
CSQP performance. 
21 
A. 
The Companies submit reports to the Commission 
22 
detailing performance on a quarterly basis.  
23 
Staff reviews these reports, whereby Staff and 
24 
1462


--- Page 135 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SCSP 
 
 
24 
 
the Companies can analyze trends and, when 
1 
necessary, employ strategies to, at a minimum, 
2 
maintain current satisfactory levels of customer 
3 
service on an ongoing basis.  At the end of the 
4 
calendar year, the Companies submit an annual 
5 
performance report, and applicable revenue 
6 
adjustments are determined.  For both KEDNY and 
7 
KEDLI, the potential adjustments are negative 
8 
only adjustments for this program.  Any 
9 
adjustments are allocated into deferral 
10 
accounts, and any balances in these accounts are 
11 
addressed in the next rate case to benefit 
12 
customers.   
13 
Q. 
Are there any additional steps Staff is taking 
14 
to review this self-reported data from the 
15 
Companies? 
16 
A. 
Yes.  In addition to the review process 
17 
discussed above, Staff is in the process of 
18 
refining additional procedures to further review 
19 
the results for the Companies’ self-reported 
20 
data.  Adding another layer of review to this 
21 
data will help both Staff and the Companies 
22 
monitor performance and consistently improve 
23 
customer service and satisfaction.  
24 
1463


--- Page 136 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SCSP 
 
 
25 
 
Q. 
What components of the CSQP are currently in 
1 
effect for KEDNY and KEDLI? 
2 
A. 
As shown in Exhibit___(SCSP-2), the current CSQP 
3 
measures for both Companies are: 1) PSC 
4 
Complaint Rate per 100,000 customers; 2) 
5 
Residential Customer Transaction Satisfaction, 
6 
measured through a customer satisfaction survey; 
7 
3) Percentage of Calls Answered within 30 
8 
Seconds; and 4) Percentage of Adjusted Customer 
9 
Bills.  As stated above, there are only negative 
10 
revenue adjustments (NRAs) associated with these 
11 
measures. 
12 
Q. 
Do the Companies file reports detailing their 
13

14 
A. 
Yes.  The Companies file quarterly and annual 
15

16

17

24 
1464


--- Page 137 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SCSP

Terminations/Uncollectibles Incentive Mechanism 
14 
Q. 
Please explain the incentive mechanism 
15 
associated with terminations and uncollectible 
16

17 
A. 
The existing terminations and uncollectibles 
18 
mechanism offers the Companies the opportunity 
19 
to earn a positive revenue adjustment (PRA) by 
20 
reducing residential customer terminations 
21 
and/or uncollectibles. 
22 
Q. 
How do the Companies define the purpose of this 
23 
metric in testimony? 
24 
1473


--- Page 146 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SCSP 
 
 
35 
 
A. 
Beginning on page 36 of the Shared Services 
1 
Panel’s direct testimony, the Companies state, 
2 
“(t)he performance measure is intended to 
3 
provide an incentive for the Companies’ to limit 
4 
the number of terminations and to address the 
5 
Commission’s interest in utilities pursuing 
6 
alternatives to terminations.” 
7 
Q. 
Do you agree with this description? 
8 
A. 
Not entirely.  While finding alternatives to 
9 
service termination and reducing residential 
10 
terminations should be among the utilities’ 
11 
goals, this definition does not fully capture 
12 
the purpose behind this mechanism.  In KEDLI and 
13 
KEDNY’s previous rate cases, 16-G-0058 and 16-G-
14 
0059, the Staff Consumer Services Panel 
15 
testified that, “(t)he Companies should be 
16 
encouraged to alter their practices to reduce 
17 
residential service terminations for nonpayment 
18 
while at the same time not increasing 
19 
uncollectibles.”  The incentive is in place to 
20 
encourage utilities to find effective ways to 
21 
lower terminations while not unduly impacting 
22 
uncollectibles.  The goal is to achieve an 
23 
appropriate balance, not to simply lower one 
24 
1474


--- Page 147 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SCSP 
 
 
36

1

upward.  The total number of customers in 
6 
arrears in 2016 was 39,909 but increased to 
7 
40,056 in 2017 to 41,656 in 2018.  The total 
8

10 
again to $19.2 million in 2018.  This mechanism 
11 
was previously introduced to encourage New 
12 
York’s utilities to find ways to lower 
13 
terminations without negatively affecting 
14 
uncollectibles; in reviewing the data since the 
15 
mechanism went into effect, the need for a more 
16 
inclusive view of the collections practices as 
17 
part of the measure has become apparent.  The 
18 
trend at both Companies in which residential 
19 
arrears are rising every year is a risk that 
20 
should be mitigated as the initiative to lower 
21 
terminations continues.  
22 
Q. 
What is the Staff Panel’s rationale for adding 
23 
this measure to the current metrics? 
24 
1484


--- Page 157 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SCSP 
 
 
46 
 
A.

1

4

6

Electronic Deferred Payment Agreements 
11 
Q. 
What is a deferred payment agreement (DPA)? 
12 
A. 
According to the Home Energy Fair Practices Act 
13 
(HEFPA), a DPA is a written agreement for the 
14 
payment of outstanding charges over a specific 
15 
period, signed by both the utility and the 
16 
customer, or applicant for utility service.  
17 
Prior to terminating service, utilities are 
18 
required to make reasonable efforts to contact 
19 
eligible customers or applicants by phone, mail, 
20 
or in person for the purpose of offering a DPA, 
21 
and to negotiate terms tailored to the 
22 
customer’s financial circumstances. 
23 
Q. 
How do the Companies currently offer DPAs to 
24 
1488


--- Page 161 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SCSP 
 
 
50 
 
customers? 
1 
A. 
The Companies’ current options for a customer to 
2 
enter into a DPA include mail, fax, email and 
3 
walk-in offices. 
4 
Q. 
What is an electronic DPA? 
5 
A. 
An electronic DPA is a written DPA that is 
6 
signed electronically by the customer.  This DPA 
7 
option provides customers with the opportunity 
8 
to negotiate a DPA over the telephone and sign 
9 
it electronically through a utility’s website.  
10 
The customer can then print the DPA and/or 
11 
receive a hard copy in the mail from the 
12 
utility. 
13 
Q. 
What value is provided by an electronic DPA? 
14 
A. 
Consistent with the intent of HEFPA, it provides 
15 
customers with an additional option to enter 
16 
into legally valid DPAs via electronic 
17 
signature.  This option makes it easier for 
18 
customers to avoid disconnections or potential 
19 
delays in restoring service and other 
20 
unnecessary hardships associated with service 
21 
termination.  The electronic DPA can be more 
22 
convenient for certain customers, and for those 
23 
who prefer to sign electronically, its adoption 
24 
1489


--- Page 162 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SCSP 
 
 
51 
 
will expedite and streamline the DPA process. 
1 
Q. 
Do any other utilities currently offer 
2 
electronic DPAs?  
3 
A. 
Yes.  The Commission has authorized the 
4 
implementation of electronic DPAs at several 
5 
utilities, such as National Fuel Gas 
6 
Distribution Corporation in Case 13-G-0016; 
7 
Niagara Mohawk in Cases 17-E-0238, et al.; and 
8 
Consolidated Edison Company of New York in Case 
9 
16-M-0501.  
10 
Q. 
Have the Companies submitted a proposal to offer 
11 
electronic DPAs to their customers? 
12 
A. 
Yes. 
13 
Q. 
What is the Panel’s recommendation regarding the 
14 
Companies’ electronic DPAs proposal? 
15 
A. 
We support the Companies’ proposal because it is 
16 
a more convenient option for customers who are 
17 
applying for and implementing a DPA, however, we 
18 
also recommend that the Companies submit, to the 
19 
Secretary, quarterly reports on the status of 
20 
electronic DPAs.  We recommend the reports 
21 
include, at a minimum, a comparison of 
22 
electronic DPAs and paper DPAs, providing the 
23 
following information for each type of 
24 
1490


--- Page 163 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SCSP 
 
 
52 
 
agreement: (1) the number of active DPAs at the 
1 
beginning of the reporting period, and the total 
2 
dollar amount; (2) the number of DPAs made 
3 
during the reporting period, and the total 
4 
dollar amount; (3) the number of DPAs reinstated 
5 
during the reporting period, and the total 
6 
dollar amount; (4) the number of DPAs defaulted 
7 
during the reporting period, and the total 
8 
dollar amount; (5) the number of DPAs satisfied 
9 
during the reporting period, and the total 
10 
dollar amount; (6) the number of active DPAs at 
11 
the end of the period, and the total dollar 
12 
amount; and (7) the percentage of DPAs in 
13 
arrears more than 60 days, in both number and 
14 
dollar amount.  These data points correspond to 
15 
information reported by all utilities for all 
16 
DPAs contained in standard collection activity 
17 
reports.  In addition, we recommend the 
18 
Commission require the Companies to include a 
19 
summary of any customer inquiries and/or 
20 
complaints received regarding electronic DPAs. 
21 
Q. 
When should the Companies file this information? 
22 
A. 
We recommend the Commission require the 
23 
Companies to file the reports with the Secretary 
24 
1491


--- Page 164 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SCSP 
 
 
53 
 
to the Commission within 30 days of the close of 
1

2

Credit/Debit Card Fees 
6 
Q. 
What is the Companies’ proposal regarding the 
7 
use of credit or debit cards? 
8 
A. 
The Companies propose that customers have the 
9 
ability to pay their utility bill directly to 
10 
the utility using credit or debit cards 
11 
(“CC/DC”).  Currently, customers may use a CC/DC 
12 
to pay their utility bills in several ways, 
13 
including on the National Grid website, the 
14 
National Grid mobile app, by telephone, and 
15 
customers enrolled in online billing may pay via 
16 
an email link that will take them to a payment 
17 
site.  The additional cost for using a CC/DC for 
18 
payment is currently $2.25 per transaction for 
19 
payment amounts between $0.00-$600.00, and $4.50 
20 
for payment amounts between $600.01-$1,200.00.  
21 
The Companies propose that the fees associated 
22 
with a CC/DC payment option be included among 
23 
the general costs of doing business, and that 
24 
1492


--- Page 165 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SCSP 
 
 
54 
 
those costs would be included in the revenue 
1 
requirement resulting in a “no-fee” model. 
2 
Q. 
Do the Companies expect CC/DC payments to rise 
3 
with the implementation of the “no-fee” model? 
4 
A. 
Yes.  The Companies state that CC/DC payments 
5 
continued to show growth from 2016 to 2017 by 
6 
10.1 percent, and that in response to surveys, 
7 
customers have indicated a preference for CC/DC 
8 
payments with no fee.  Additionally, the 
9 
Companies stated that based on an analysis 
10 
involving National Grid’s third-party payment 
11 
processor and the Companies’ current volumes of 
12 
CC/DC payments, an increase of CC/DC payments of 
13 
53 percent is expected with the no-fee model in 
14 
the Rate Year. 
15 
Q. 
What are the Companies’ proposed costs for this 
16 
program? 
17 
A. 
As stated in the Companies’ Shared Services 
18 
Panel direct testimony, the Rate Year costs 
19 
would be $1.964 million for KEDNY and $0.625 
20 
million for KEDLI based on the existing per-
21 
transaction costs.  The Companies propose to 
22 
fully reconcile these costs.  
23 
Q. 
What are the Panel’s recommendations to the 
24 
1493


--- Page 166 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SCSP 
 
 
55 
 
Commission regarding the Companies’ CC/DC 
1 
proposal? 
2 
A. 
The Panel recommends that the Commission allow 
3 
the Companies to accept CC/DC directly from 
4 
customers for bill payment, under the condition 
5 
that the per-transaction level does not exceed 
6 
the current amount of $2.25 as described in the 
7 
Companies’ response to DPS-368.  We also 
8 
recommend the Commission impose a downward-only 
9 
reconciliation, so the Companies are 
10 
incentivized to keep these fees at a reasonable 
11 
level in the Rate Year.   
12 
Q. 
Why is the Panel recommending this? 
13 
A. 
Staff recognizes that customers are increasingly 
14 
using CC/DC to make purchases and that a per-
15 
transaction vendor fee to pay a utility bill is 
16 
inconvenient to customers, as most businesses 
17 
include this as a cost of doing business.  
18 
However, the Panel is concerned that the 
19 
transaction fees, once socialized in base rates, 
20 
will heavily increase, as is the case at other 
21 
New York utilities; therefore, we recommend the 
22 
Commission direct the Companies to use its 
23 
aggregate buying power to lower this per-
24 
1494


--- Page 167 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
SCSP 
 
 
56 
 
transaction amount to ensure that customers are 
1 
protected. 
2 
Q. 
Does the Panel recommend any reporting 
3 
requirements for this new program? 
4 
A. 
Yes, the Panel recommends that the Commission 
5 
require the Companies to submit quarterly 
6 
reports to the Secretary evaluating associated 
7 
program fees and the per-transaction rate, as 
8 
well as customer participation, in comparison 
9 
with the Companies’ other payment methods, such 
10 
as walk-in offices, mail, direct debit and 
11 
Western Union.  In these reports, we recommend 
12 
the Commission require the Companies to provide 
13 
justification for any 10% increase in the 
14 
budget.

What is the purpose of your rebuttal testimony? 
5 
A. 
The purpose of our testimony is to rebut the New 
6 
York City Low Income Panel’s (NYC LIP) testimony 
7 
addressing its proposed modifications to The 
8 
Brooklyn Union Gas Company d/b/a National Grid 
9

10

11 
Companies) Gas Affordability Programs, which 
12 
would require that the Commission return to 
13 
setting low income discounts and budgets in 
14 
individual rate cases and is contrary to the 
15 
Commission’s current policy. 
16 
Q. 
Where did the Commission set out its current 
17 
policy for setting low income discounts? 
18 
A. 
The Commission conducted a generic proceeding, 
19 
Case 14-M-0565 (the Affordability Proceeding), 
20 
setting forth its policy and methodology for 
21 
setting low income discounts.  Specifically, the 
22 
Commission did this in two orders: the Order 
23 
Adopting Low Income Program Modifications and 
24 
1509

Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 SCSP REBUTTAL 
 
2 
 
Directing Utility Filings, issued on May 20,

4 
issued on February 17, 2017 (February 2017 
5

6 
established a tiered level discount framework 
7 
based on a level of need and each level is 
8 
unique, which is determined by a formulaic 
9 
approach.  The discounts are further stratified 
10 
by heating and non-heating participants for each 
11 
service type.  Additionally, the Commission set 
12 
a cap on the aggregate cost of the discounts, of 
13 
two percent of a utility’s total revenues. 
14 
Q. 
How is a program budget determined under the 
15

16 
A. 
The budget is mainly a function of the size of 
17 
the eligible population, and the size of the 
18 
discount.  As set forth on pages 29 through 32 
19 
in the May 2016 Order, budgets set in rate cases 
20 
are estimates because participation levels and 
21 
discount amounts may vary over the course of a 
22 
rate year or rate plan.  To account for this, 
23 
budgets are reconciled with actual program costs 
24 
1510


--- Page 183 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 SCSP REBUTTAL 
 
3 
 
using traditional deferral accounting 
1 
procedures.

8

16

18

20

21

22

23 
sought to standardize these programs among 
24 
1514


--- Page 187 ---
Cases 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 SCSP REBUTTAL 
 
7 
 
utilities to ensure that there is a more 
1

3

and in the Commission’s Affordability 
9

15

an annual basis.

2 
 
Introduction and Overview 
1 
Q.  
Please state your name, business address, and identify for whom you are presenting 
2 
testimony in these proceedings. 
3 
A. 
My name is William D. Yates, my office address is at Public Utility Law Project of New 
4 
York, Inc., 90 South Swan Street - Suite 305, Albany, NY 12210.  I am presenting 
5 
testimony in these proceedings for the Public Utility Law Project of New York, Inc. 
6 
(“PULP”). 
7 
Q.  
Please describe PULP and your relationship to the organization.  
8 
A. 
PULP is a New York not-for-profit corporation that was formed in 1981.  Its primary focus 
9 
is to promote and defend the legal rights of residential utility consumers by educating the 
10 
public, regulators and elected officials about the impacts of utility rates, conducting 
11 
research on the rights and energy burden of utility consumers, and advocacy with an 
12 
emphasis on the rights and needs of low-income utility consumers.  I have been employed 
13 
by PULP in various capacities since July of 1990.  I am currently Director of Research for 
14 
PULP. 
15 
Q.  
What is your educational background, your professional qualifications, and 
16 
employment history? 
 
17 
A. 
I am a graduate of Colgate University (B.A. in History, 1982) and a graduate of the New 
18 
York University Stern School of Business Administration (M.S. in Accounting, 1987).  I 
19 
am a Certified Public Accountant (CPA), licensed to practice in New York State since 
20 
1987, and I am a member of the American Institute of Certified Public Accountants 
21 
(AICPA). 
22 
Q.  
Have you previously testified before the New York State Public Service Commission? 
23 
A. 
Yes, I have provided testimony before the Public Service Commission (“PSC” or 
24 
“Commission”) on behalf of PULP in a number of prior proceedings, including the 
25 
following cases in 2012, 2013, and from 2016 through the present:

Q.  
What is the purpose of your testimony in these proceedings? 
17 
A. 
My testimony provides evidence of and examines and offers recommendations in several 
18 
areas: 
19 
1. Evidence that, based on U.S. Census Bureau data, approximately two-thirds of low 
20 
income households in the Companies’ service areas spent at least 50% of their 
21 
income on housing costs, including utilities, (a severe “Housing Cost Burden”) by 
22 
2017; while the percent of moderate income households in the KEDNY/KEDLI 
23 
service area suffering a severe Housing Cost Burden rose in all counties/boroughs 
24 
of the combined service areas from 2014 - 2017. I estimate that approximately 
25 
305,000 low income households and 71,000 moderate income households with 
26 
severe Housing Cost Burdens (a total of 376,000 households) are residential 
27 
1610


--- Page 283 ---
CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
 
       TESTIMONY OF WILLIAM D. YATES, CPA 
 
5 
 
KEDNY/KEDLI customers, which was based on the methodology I used and 
1 
describe in the body of my testimony. 
2 
2. Evidence that 14% of KEDNY residential customers and 9% of KEDLI residential 
3 
customers are more than 60 days late paying their bills and are falling further and 
4 
further behind. Both the total dollars owed, and the amount owed per KEDNY 
5 
customer have been in a strong uptrend since 2017. In the twelve months ending 
6 
June 30, 2019 alone, the arrears of all KEDNY residential customers rose 15%. 
7 
3. Evidence that KEDNY and KEDLI’s’ proposed rate increases – which they state 
8 
would increase gas delivery rates by 9.61% and 6.15%, respectively, do not include 
9 
the bill impact of the earnings adjustment mechanisms (EAMs) proposed.  
10 
Similarly, the Companies do not quantify the impact on bills of the increasing 
11 
likelihood that significant additional capital investments and subsidies for 
12 
alternatives to gas heating, gas efficiency programs and demand reduction 
13 
programs will be needed as the Companies struggle with a hard moratorium on new 
14 
gas service in both KEDNY and KEDLI’s service territories. 
15 
4. Evidence that KEDNY has also failed to consider the bill impact of a Site 
16 
Investigation and Remediation (SIR) Recovery Surcharge that could add 2% per 
17 
year to residential customers’ bills on a total bill basis for decades. 
18 
5. Evidence that while KEDNY has had significant success enrolling residential 
19 
customers in its EAP who may be eligible, KEDLI has not been able to achieve 
20 
comparable results. U.S. Census Bureau data strongly suggest only 20 - 30% of 
21 
KEDLI potentially eligible households enrolled, compared to 70 – 90% for 
22 
KEDNY. 
23 
6. Evidence that KEDNY’s collection procedures do not  ensure  that  residential  
24 
customers  having difficulty  paying  their  bills  are  offered  affordable  deferred  
25 
payment  agreements (DPAs) meeting  the  statutory  requirements  set  forth  by  
26 
the  Home  Energy  Fair  Practices  Act (HEFPA). 
27 
7. Evidence that public assistance (PA) customers who encounter difficulties with 
28 
payments and notifications by the New York City Human Resources 
29 
1611

CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310

Administration (HRA) and the Nassau County and Suffolk County departments of 
1 
social services (Nassau/Suffolk DSS) applicable to their KEDNY and KEDLI

3

Q.  
Please provide a brief overview of the sources you reviewed that led you to make your 
20 
findings and form your recommendations. 
21 
A. 
As discussed in more detail in the remainder of my testimony, I reviewed information from 
22 
several sources that provided evidence that most of the Companies’ low income customers 
23 
cannot afford their utility bills, and that the population of low income customers is much 
24 
larger than the existing and future anticipated number of participants in the Companies’ 
25 
expanded Energy Affordability Plans (EAPs).  Sources included testimony by the 
26

Residential Affordability 
1 
Bill Impacts of Companies’ Proposed Rate Increases 
2 
Q.  
Please describe the Companies’ proposed increases in residential rates. 
3 
A. According to the Companies’ filing letter, a “typical” KEDNY residential heating customer 
4 
would experience a $16.66 monthly increase, equating to an 11.99% increase in their bill. 
5 
A typical KEDLI residential heating customer will see a $7.14 monthly increase, equating 
6 
to a 5.15% increase in their bill. However, as the filing letter also indicates, 
7 
“…the effect on individual customer’s monthly bills will vary depending on the 
8 
customer’s usage and service classification.” 
9 
(Filing Letter at 3) 
10 
In their July 3, 2019 filing corrections and updates, KEDNY and KEDLI indicated that 
11 
typical residential heating customers would experience increases of $12.93 and $8.16, or 
12 
9.61% and 6.15% on their total monthly bills, respectively.  (KEDNY Exhibit ___(RDP-
13 
4CU), Schedule 4 at 2); (Exhibit ___(RDP-4CU), Schedule 4 at 3). Total monthly bill 
14 
increases factor in changes in cost of the supply of natural gas in addition to the delivery 
15 
costs to provide the service, which renders the “total bill” increase noticeably lower than 
16 
the delivery costs increases. Since the delivery cost is totally under the control of the 
17 
Companies, whereas supply costs are not, delivery cost is a more accurate measure of the 
18 
increases levied by the Companies upon their customers. 
19 
Q. 
What is a “typical” residential heating customer, and what are the equivalent 
20 
increases in the delivery charge portion for such customers? 
21 
A. 
For both KEDNY and KEDLI, a typical residential heating customer is one whose average 
22 
usage is 83 therms per month, or approximately 1,000 therms annually.  The delivery bill 
23 
increase for a typical KEDNY residential heating customer would be 13.93% under the 
24 
Companies’ proposal. (Exhibit ___(RDP-4CU), Schedule 4 at 2).  For KEDLI, the average 
25 
1621

CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310

16 
 
usage customer would experience an 8.64% increase in delivery charges. (Exhibit 
1 
___(RDP-4CU), Schedule 4 at 3). 
2

3

For KEDNY customers with average monthly usage less than 83 therms, the percentage of

4CU), Schedule 4 at 2).

4CU), Schedule 4 at 3).

on both a delivery bill and total bill basis. The average KEDNY non-heating customer uses

Q. 
Why are the proposed increases higher in percentage terms for EAP customers than 
13 
non-EAP customers? 
14 
A. 
Participants in the KEDNY and KEDLI energy assistance programs receive a monthly 
15 
fixed discount on their bills. For purposes of reporting the bill impacts of the Companies’ 
16 
proposed rate increases, each has used the minimum discount available to EAP customers, 
17 
which is $18 for heating and $3 for non-heating. These fixed discounts amplify the 
18 
percentage impact of the Companies’ proposed increases on EAP participants, which are 
19 
the same in dollar terms but are larger when expressed as a percentage of EAP customers’ 
20 
typical bills than those of customers who are not EAP participants. 
21 
Q. 
Are there any adjustments to EAP plan discounts to mitigate the percentage impact 
22 
of the Companies’ proposed increases on EAP customers? 
23 
A. 
Not directly.  Pursuant to the orders in Case 14-M-0565 that created a statewide framework 
24 
for the administration of utility low income customer assistance programs in New York, 
25 
each utility is required annually to update the amount of the discounts it provides to 
26 
1623


--- Page 296 ---
CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
 
       TESTIMONY OF WILLIAM D. YATES, CPA 
 
18 
 
program participants.1  The update process is formulaic, setting a single (fixed) discount 
1 
amount for each “tier” within the utility’s program.  Discounts are therefore not based on 
2 
individual customer bills (i.e. as a percentage of the total bill) and do not provide an 
3 
adjustment mechanism tailored to individual customer bills sufficient to mitigate the 
4 
impact of rate increases proportionately for all customers. For example, in the proposed 
5 
Rate Year proposed for these rate plans, each Tier 1 heating EAP customer will receive an 
6 
$18 monthly discount (up $1 from the previous discount of $17), regardless of their total 
7 
bill. (Shared Services Panel Testimony at 72).  Tier 1 customers with total bill increases of 
8 
$10 resulting from the Companies’ proposals would see 10% of such increases offset ($1 
9 
divided by $10); while Tier 1 customers whose total bills increased by $20 would only 
10 
achieve mitigation of their increases of 5% ($1 divided by $20). 
11 
Q.  
Have the Companies proposed any changes to the enrollment tiers in the EAP for 
12 
either KEDNY or KEDLI? 
13 
A.  
Yes. The Companies are proposing to eliminate Tier 5 for KEDLI and Tiers 5 and 6 for 
14 
KEDNY, thereby transitioning those customers into Tier 1. (Shared Services Panel 
15 
Testimony at 74).  The Companies’ rationale for these changes is that certain tiers are 
16 
duplicative.   
17

18 
customers? 
19

Yes. KEDNY proposes to increase its basic service charges for heating gas service by

$1.85, or 8.58%, to $23.40 and to raise its basic service charges for non-heating $2.40, or 
21 
14.77%, to $18.65; while KEDLI proposes to increase its basic service charges by $1.64 
22 
(7.57%) for heating and $2.80 (14.18%) for non-heating, respectively. The resulting basic 
23 
service charges for KEDNY would be $23.40 and $18.65 for heating and non-heating,

(2) Site Investigation and Remediation (SIR) Recovery Surcharge 
4

5

Newtown Creek in residential ratepayer bill impacts. The Company estimates non-

superfund “site investigation and remediation” (SIR) costs for Rate Year 1 and Data Years 
8 
1-3 to be $66.1, $62.6, $44.9 and $30.0 million, respectively. (Testimony of Charles F.

11

12

13

15

6

7 
including any “dead band,” to reduce deferred Site Investigation and Remediation (SIR) 
8 
costs. 
9 
Q. 
What is an “earnings-sharing mechanism”? 
10 
A. 
An earnings-sharing mechanism (ESM) is a pre-determined way of providing for the 
11 
sharing of any potential earnings between ratepayers and the utility that are above specified 
12 
return on equity (ROE) thresholds set in a joint proposal or by Commission order. For 
13 
example, the Commission’s order in Case 16-G-0058 adopted an ESM whereby KEDNY 
14 
would keep 100% of any earnings above the 9.0% authorized ROE up to 9.5% (the “dead 
15 
band”). Thereafter, 50% of any earnings exceeding 9.5% but less than or equal to 10%; 
16 
75% of any earnings exceeding 10% but less than or equal to 10%; and 90% of any earnings 
17 
exceeding 10.5% were designated to be deferred for the benefit of customers.7 
18 
Q. 
Is there any precedent for utilizing the utility’s share of earnings above the authorized 
19 
ROE to pay down deferred SIR costs? 
20 
A. 
Yes. In Case 11-M-0034, a generic proceeding initiated by concerned intervenors focused 
21 
on the allocation criteria of SIR costs between shareholders and ratepayers, the 
22 
Commission declined to adopt a generic policy requiring sharing or reallocation of SIR 
23 
costs between utility ratepayers and shareholders, but identified two circumstances in 
24 
which sharing of SIR costs may be appropriate: 
25 
 
7 See: Order Adopting Terms Of Joint Proposal and Establishing Gas Rate Plans, Case 16-G-0058 at 34-35. 
Available at: http://documents.dps.ny.gov/public/Common/ViewDoc.aspx?DocRefId={A75F304F-604B-4ECA-
A3B8-596DCAE1F72D}.

• Where utilities have failed to adequately constrain SIR costs, or; 
1 
• As part of multiyear rate plans incorporating excess earnings mechanisms 8 
 
2 
Additionally, in Cases 13-E-0030 and 13-G-0031, the Commission accepted the proposal 
3 
of the settling parties to adopt an ESM designating 50% of Con Edison’s share of earnings 
4 
exceeding the “dead band” to be used to reduce deferred SIR costs. In the case at hand, 
5 
KEDNY reasonably anticipates a multi-year settlement and an excess earning mechanism. 
6 
One of the Commission’s two preconditions for sharing of SIR costs between the utility 
7 
and ratepayers identified above is therefore met.

14

15 
attended by PULP, customers shared their experiences of “temporarily” suspending their 
16 
gas service to make renovations to their homes and businesses who were later denied 
17

18

20 
gas service. (Public Statement Hearing Brooklyn, July 31, 2019, pages 58-60).

Q.  
Taken as a whole, can residential customers of KEDNY/KEDLI afford the increased 
1 
rates, added surcharges and other bill impacts proposed by the Companies? 
2 
A. 
No. For the Companies’ low-to-moderate income residential (LMI) customers, the 
3 
Companies’ proposed rate increases would place a heavy burden on these already 
4 
financially challenged households, especially considering the simultaneous rate increase 
5 
request by Con Edison in the KEDNY service area. 
6 
Taken together, the impact of these unquantified increases, surcharges and other bill 
7 
impacts increases KEDNY and KEDLI’s effective increases on a delivery bill basis to 
8 
17.8% and 9.6%, respectively, for the proposed initial rate year alone14. These percentage 
9 
increases far exceed historic rates of CPI and other measures of cost of living growth, as 
10 
well as the past decade’s average annual rate of increase in household income in the 
11 
Companies’ service areas. As will be shown, the Companies’ proposed increases are well 
12 
beyond anything that their LMI ratepayers can afford. 
13 
Q. 
How are LMI customers defined? 
14 
A. 
There is no single definition of a “low income” or “moderate income” utility customer in 
15 
New York State. However, in recent years policies have been set forth by State agencies 
16 
such as the Public Service Commission (PSC) and the New York State Energy Research 
17 
and Development Authority (NYSERDA) that define “low income” customers as 
18 
households whose annual income is at or below 60% of the statewide median household 
19 
income and “moderate income” customers as households whose annual income is greater 
20 
than 60% and less than or equal to 80% of the statewide median income. For example, in 
21 
Case 14-M-0465, the PSC determined that utilities should develop low income 
22 
affordability programs with the goal of achieving a 6% energy burden for households 
23 
whose annual income is at or below 60% of the statewide median income.15 And in April 
24

rates) + 1.00% (EAMs) + ? (Moratorium) = 9.64%.

5 
Housing Cost Burdens 
6 
Q. 
Please describe the economic challenges that LMI customers in the Companies’ 
7 
service areas experience. 
8 
A. 
Since the Financial Crisis and Great Recession of 2008 – 2010, the biggest economic 
9 
challenge faced by LMI customers has been that household incomes in this sector have not 
10 
kept up with rising housing costs, which include utilities. This phenomenon was not new 
11 
to the post-2010 New York State economy; but was made much more acute by substantial 
12 
increases in rental housing demand and weakening incomes that increased competition for 
13 
already-scarce affordable units following the Great Recession.17 One indication of the 
14 
growing disparity is the large gap between the compound annual growth rates of median 
15 
household income and median gross rent since 2010 in the Companies’ service areas, as 
16 
shown in Chart 2. 
17 
 
16 See: New Efficiency: New York at 8. Available at: https://www.nyserda.ny.gov/About/Publications/New-Efficiency. 
17 See: Worst Case Housing Needs 2011: Report to Congress, U.S. Department of Housing and Urban Development, 
February 2013. Available at: https://www.huduser.gov/portal/publications/affhsg/wc_HsgNeeds11.html.

2

3 
for LMI customers. The U.S. Department of Housing and Urban Development (HUD) 
4

5 
“those ‘who pay more than 30 percent of their income for housing’ and ‘may have 
6 
difficulty affording necessities such as food, clothing, transportation, and medical 
7 
care.’ Severe rent burden is defined as paying more than 50 percent of one’s income 
8 
on rent.”18 
9 
Since 2014, the U.S. Census Bureau has included in its American Community Survey 
10 
(ACS) a more comprehensive measure of severe “Housing Cost Burdens” comprised of 
11 
both owner and renter households who spend at least fifty percent (50%) of their annual 
12 
incomes on housing costs, including utilities.19  Analyzing these data for LMI households 
13 
in the Companies’ service areas between 2014 and 2017, as shown in Charts 3 and 4, 
14 
reveals just how challenging and persistent the Housing Cost Burden has become for this 
15 
sector, particularly for low income households.

and quarterly reports for 2018 – 2019 reflect the large increase in enrollments thus far.25,26 
2

3

4

5 
EAP enrollments.  To date, KEDLI has experienced under-enrollment, largely due to the 
6 
fact that it does not have a matching program for Nassau County and Suffolk County 
7 
(Nassau/Suffolk) equivalent in functionality to KEDNY’s matching program for New York 
8 
City.  
9 
Apart from any beneficial offset to rate increases that might occur through increased 
10 
participation in the Companies’ EAP programs, there is no other way that these programs 
11 
will provide an offset to low income customer bills.  As discussed previously, discount 
12 
levels will not have materially increased to take account of proposed rate increases, so 
13 
existing participants will experience bill impacts just like all other residential customers of 
14 
the Companies.  In fact – as also stated previously – rate increases for EAP customers will 
15 
be higher in percentage terms, since these customers receive a fixed discount on their bills. 
16

Q. 
At what point do increasing arrears become a problem for the Companies? 
1 
A. 
They are already a problem. The Companies’ Shared Services Panel Testimony has this to 
2 
say about the interplay between service terminations, accounts receivable and 
3

4 
“The short-term effect of reducing terminations is to suppress uncollectible write 
5 
off. However, the long-term effect of reducing terminations is to increase accounts 
6 
receivable, which with all else equal, in turn leads to higher uncollectible write offs 
7 
when these accounts with larger receivable balances are eventually written off. If 
8 
efforts to further reduce terminations for the Companies continue, accounts 
9 
receivable will continue to rise (with potentially higher write off).” 
10 
(Shared Services Panel Testimony at 32)

expense.28

16

17

18

20 
34,638. It is therefore time to reset the positive targets for the metric, and to also add to it 
21 
a negative revenue adjustment (NRA) that would address any risk that a sudden spike in 
22 
terminations might erode the progress that has been made in reducing terminations in recent 
23 
years. 
24

25

26 
the period. (Exhibit ___(WDY-5) at 6). KEDLI has therefore also achieved a positive 
27 
revenue adjustment for exceeding its target in each of the years of its current rate plan, 
28 
1641


--- Page 314 ---
CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
 
       TESTIMONY OF WILLIAM D. YATES, CPA 
 
36 
 
during which time terminations dropped well below its target of 12,470. It is therefore also 
1 
time to reset the positive targets for KEDLI’s metric, and to also add to it a negative revenue 
2 
adjustment (NRA) that would address any risk that a sudden spike in KEDLI terminations

Q.  
What do you recommend for a modified terminations and uncollectibles metric? 
5 
A. 
PRA targets for incentive mechanisms should be established that challenge normal 
6 
performance.  They should be so-called “stretch” or “reach” targets. Moreover, the 
7 
incentive should operate in this framework with a counterbalancing NRA to provide a 
8 
disincentive to high rates of service termination. My targets, which are the result of the 
9 
analysis I performed on Pages 4 - 7 of Exhibit ___(WDY-05) - Corrected are set two 
10 
standard deviations above and below the most recent normalized seven-year average of 
11 
terminations for the twelve months ending June 30th. To normalize the data, I excluded the 
12 
highest and lowest years from the averages. The results for KEDNY are: Terminations - 
13 
PRA target of 27,206 / NRA target of 35,386; Uncollectibles - PRA target of $10,962,806 
14 
/ NRA target of $13,635,112; Arrears - PRA target of $53,869,055 / NRA target of 
15 
$62,734,400. For KEDLI, the targets are: Terminations – PRA 8,912 / NRA 13,249; 
16 
Uncollectibles – PRA $3,784,231 / NRA $9,590,247; Arrears - PRA $20,055,915 / NRA 
17 
$36,807,173.          
18 
To ensure that the most recent data serves as the basis for the ultimate targets for a modified 
19 
terminations and collectibles metric, I also recommend that the targets be updated to reflect 
20 
the latest available as of the effective date of any order in these proceedings.  
21 
Q.  
Why did you use two standard deviations when calculating the targets? 
22 
A. 
Statistically, a result that is more than two standard deviations from the average carries a 
23 
95 percent confidence level that the result is not due to normal, random variations. Such a 
24 
result therefore is most likely due to utility actions. For this reason, all of the targets 
25 
established in the above cases are based on the same standard. 
26 
1642

Proposals/Recommendations 
1 
Q. 
In addition to the recommendations you have made thus far, do you have any 
2 
proposals that could reduce the impact of the Companies’ proposed rate increases on 
3 
LMI customers? 
4 
A. 
Yes. Broadly speaking, the parties in these proceedings should endeavor to find the means 
5 
to limit the total bill impacts of any rate increases for typical (average usage) residential 
6 
customers to no more than two percent (2.0%) per rate year. Additionally, the parties 
7 
should explore alternative rate designs that would lower basic service charges for 
8 
residential customers and, more generally, reward conservation and energy efficiency 
9 
through lower delivery bills. While such measures are sensible and promote the public 
10 
interest in affordability and responsible environmental stewardship, increased gas 
11 
efficiency and conservation-oriented rates are particularly compelling in the shadow the 
12 
Companies’ gas moratoria in their service territories, and the State’s intermediate and long-
13 
term plans to reduce its carbon footprint. Toward these objectives, I offer the following 
14 
recommendations for initiatives that could contribute to such achieving limited rate 
15 
increases:  
16
