Q. 
Do you agree with the Company “Affordability Metric for Low Income Customers” 
27 
as proposed? 
28 
1647


--- Page 320 ---
CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
 
       TESTIMONY OF WILLIAM D. YATES, CPA 
 
42 
 
A. 
No.  The Companies propose an outcome-oriented energy efficiency metric measuring the 
1 
annual percentage reduction in EAP customer bills, but the “outcome” they propose would 
2 
not necessarily be reflective of efforts KEDNY and KEDLI should be taking to reduce 
3 
individual EAP customer bills, such as those just discussed. Instead, the proposed outcome 
4 
is simply a year-over-year reduction in the weather-normalized average monthly customer 
5 
bill for EAP customers. Based only upon such a reduction, the Companies propose that 
6 
they be awarded one basis point for each 0.2 percent reduction in the average EAP customer 
7 
bill achieved within their respective service territories, up to a total award of 10 basis points 
8 
for the incentive. (Future of Heat Testimony at 106-7) This would result in up to $3.3 
9 
million and $2.1 million in added revenue for KEDNY and KEDLI, respectively, derived 
10 
from an EAM that I believe is ineffective and inappropriate, as I explain below. 
11 
Q. 
Why are reduced average monthly customer bills not an appropriate outcome to use 
12 
for measuring the effectiveness of such a metric? 
13 
A. 
Using this outcome is problematic for at least two reasons, namely: 
14 
• If a significant number of higher usage EAP customers had their service terminated 
15 
in any given year, that would tend to reduce average monthly bills but not be 
16 
reflective of improved affordability for continuing EAP customers. The opposite 
17 
could also be true for any year in which terminated high usage EAP customers had 
18 
their service reconnected; and  
19 
• Various mixes of enrollments and dis-enrollments could impact average EAP 
20 
customer monthly bills and trigger a potential award of added revenue, but not be 
21 
reflective of improved affordability for continuing EAP customers. 
22 
Q. 
What do you suggest instead? 
23 
A. 
Incentives should embody “stretch” goals that go above-and-beyond the performance 
24 
expected of the Companies. Such targets are readily discernible from the cost-benefit 
25 
analyses KEDNY and KEDLI provided to support their proposals for the LMI Gas 
26 
Conversion Programs and HEAT programs.  With that in mind, I propose targets for the 
27 
proposed affordability metric be Participant Energy Cost Reductions in excess of 
28 
1648


--- Page 321 ---
CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
 
       TESTIMONY OF WILLIAM D. YATES, CPA 
 
43 
 
$8,421,139.87 for KEDNY and $6,075,243.86 for KEDLI that achieving at least the same 
1 
SCT Ratios — (Participant Energy Cost Reductions + Avoided CO2 Emissions) / Program 
2 
Administration Costs —  1.73 for KEDNY; 1.11 for KEDLI. (Exhibit ___ (FOH-2), 
3 
Schedules 5 and 6). 
4 
I do not propose that the affordability metric be based on the HEAT programs however, 
5 
since the KEDLI program already includes an incentive metric, and presumably any 
6 
equivalent KEDNY program would include the same. 
7 
The Companies Should Accelerate The Return To Customers of Excess Unprotected Accumulated 
8 
Deferred Federal Income Taxes Resulting From The 2017 Tax Cut And Jobs Act  
9 
Q. 
What is “Excess Unprotected Accumulated Deferred Federal Income Tax”? 
10 
A. 
Accumulated Deferred Federal Income Tax, or “ADFIT”, is a balance sheet item 
11 
representing the historic differences between federal income tax expense based on 
12 
generally accepted accounting principles (“GAAP” or “Book” Basis) and federal income 
13 
tax liability as calculated when preparing a federal income tax return (Tax Basis). GAAP 
14 
Basis deferred taxes are usually net liabilities on the balance sheet; that is, they represent 
15 
taxes to be paid in the future as timing differences between GAAP and Tax Basis 
16 
accounting resolve to a zero balance. 
17 
Significant amounts of the Companies’ accumulated deferred federal income tax (ADFIT) 
18 
liabilities are the result of decades of capital investments in property for which accelerated 
19 
depreciation has been available to be taken when determining Tax Basis income tax 
20 
liability. Until 2017, GAAP Basis deferred corporate federal income taxes were accrued at 
21 
the then applicable corporate rate of thirty five percent (35%). As part of the 2017 Tax Cut 
22 
and Jobs Act (TCJA), however, the federal corporate tax rate was lowered to twenty-one 
23 
percent (21%). For GAAP purposes, this reduction in the tax rate had the effect of requiring 
24 
a substantial reduction of the Companies’ ADFIT balances sheet since approximately forty 
25 
percent (40%) of these liabilities would no longer need to be paid in the future.32  
26 
 
32 35% - 21% = 14%.  14% / 35% = 40%. 
1649


--- Page 322 ---
CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
 
       TESTIMONY OF WILLIAM D. YATES, CPA 
 
44 
 
For most corporations, the effect of reducing ADFIT balances (creating “Excess ADFIT”) 
1 
was a windfall that could be returned to owners. The situation for “cost of service” 
2 
regulated utilities was different however, in that utility rates have historically included an 
3 
allowance for income tax expense on a GAAP Basis. (In effect, ratepayers have “prepaid” 
4 
utilities’ income taxes through the rates they have paid over time in their monthly utility 
5 
bills.) In light of this “prepayment” aspect of setting utility rates in New York State, the 
6 
Public Service Commission ruled in Case 17-M-0815, that reductions of utility ADFIT 
7 
balances should be preserved for the net benefit to ratepayers, rather than allowing a 
8 
windfall for utility shareholders. With regard to KEDNY and KEDLI’s “Excess ADFIT”, 
9 
the Commission decided that the method and timing of returning excess ADFIT to 
10 
ratepayers should be determined in the Companies’ next rate cases.33 
11 
Excess ADFIT has two components: “Protected” and “Unprotected” balances.  Protected 
12 
Excess ADFIT represents those balances related to accelerated depreciation taken on 
13 
capital investment in property; whereas Unprotected ADFIT represents — in the case of 
14 
the Companies — represents the differences between the book and tax basis of deductions 
15 
taken for property (plant) repairs, contributions in aid of construction, and other temporary 
16 
differences such as the current tax deduction for cost of removal spending. (Revenue 
17 
Requirements Panel Testimony at 97).  Protected Excess ADFIT is subject to special IRS 
18 
“normalization” rules that generally do not permit the return of excess balances faster than 
19 
the GAAP amortization of the underlying assets. However, Unprotected Excess ADFIT – 
20 
balances related to repairs, etc. – is not subject to IRS normalization rules and, in the case 
21 
of KEDNY and KEDLI, may be returned to ratepayers without restriction as to timing. In 
22 
other words, KEDNY and KEDLI could return these funds to ratepayers immediately, 
23 
which would act to moderate the effect of the Companies’ proposed rate increases.          
24 
Q. 
What timing have the Companies proposed for returning Unprotected Excess ADFIT 
25 
balances for the net benefit of ratepayers? 
26 
 
33 See: Order Determining Rate Treatment of Tax Changes, Case 17-M-0815 at 45-46. Available at: 
http://documents.dps.ny.gov/public/Common/ViewDoc.aspx?DocRefId={26E713D4-DB52-4A26-BF4F-
7FD7B9E5C1AC}. 
1650


--- Page 323 ---
CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
 
       TESTIMONY OF WILLIAM D. YATES, CPA 
 
45 
 
A. 
KEDNY and KEDLI have proposed that their respective Unprotected Excess ADFIT be 
1 
returned over the average remaining book life of the net book value of plant assets. 
2 
(KEDNY Revenue Requirements Panel at 97-98; KEDLI Revenue Requirements Panel at 
3 
95-96). 
4 
Q. 
Do you agree with the Companies’ proposals? 
5 
A. 
No.  The KEDNY and KEDLI Unprotected Excess ADFIT balances are $157.1 million 
6 
and $62.5 million, respectively. The average remaining book life of the net book value of 
7 
plant assets is 44 years for KEDNY and 47 years for KEDLI. (KEDNY, KEDLI Exhibits 
8 
__ (RRP-11), Workpapers to Exhibit ___ (RRP-6), Workpaper 2, Page 1). The Companies 
9 
are proposing to return the benefit of these assets (which represent excess taxes prepaid by 
10 
ratepayers) over amortization periods that bear no relationship to IRS requirements for such 
11 
balances.  In fact, these ratepayer funds could and should be returned to the ratepayers 
12 
immediately.  Instead, the Companies propose to extend the amortization period of these 
13 
balances to almost half a century while at the same time proposing large rate increases 
14 
which, as I explained previously, cannot be afforded by 221,000 to 305,000 (30% to 40%) 
15 
of KEDLI/KEDNY low income customers. (Exhibit ___(WDY-5) at 1). 
16 
Q. 
Do you have an alternative proposal? 
17 
A. 
Yes. The entirety of Unprotected Excess ADFIT balances should be used during the rate 
18 
period determined in these proceedings to offset the bill impacts of the Companies’ large 
19 
proposed rate increases. These funds belong to customers and should be made available 
20 
now to mitigate any such increases, rather than retained on the Companies’ balance sheets. 
21 
Many of KEDNY and KEDLI’s residential customers have prepaid these excess amounts 
22 
for decades through their utility bills. Many if not most of those customers will likely not 
23 
be alive for another 44 – 47 years to realize the benefit of the return of their excessive 
24 
prepaid rates, as proposed by the Companies. This is the customers’ money – it should be 
25 
returned to them during the rate plan to offset rate increases, and seniors and other 
26 
ratepayers should receive the benefits of their pre-payments, rather than being subjected to 
27 
increases that are many times greater than increases in social security. 
28 
1651

CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310

2 
Tables 1 and 2 also show that problems with financial statement forms were also among 
3 
the top 10 reasons for residential customer complaints for each company.  
4 
Q.  
What is a financial statement form and why is it important? 
5 
A.   
A financial statement form (FSF) is a form that a utility may require an eligible residential 
6 
customer to complete who is requesting more lenient terms than the Company’s “standard 
7 
offer”, which typically requires a 15% down payment of past due arrears, with the 
8 
remaining balance to be paid over a 10-month period. FSF disclosures include a customer’s 
9 
assets, income and expenses. The utility may require reasonable substantiation of the 
10 
information on that form, provided that all such information shall be treated as confidential. 
11 
(16 NYCRR § 11.10(a)(1)(ii)). 
12 
FSFs are important because they provide an objective view of a customer’s individual 
13 
financial circumstances: in particular, whether they have “positive cash flow” and therefore 
14 
can afford to make payments toward his or her utility arrears; or have “negative cash flow” 
15 
and therefore already spend more than he or she receives and may only be able to make 
16 
nominal repayments.  Basing the terms of a DPA on the results of a completed FSF is one 
17 
way that a utility can ensure that it has negotiated DPA terms tailored to the customer’s 
18 
financial circumstances, as required by NYCRR § 11.10. 
19 
1654


--- Page 327 ---
CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
 
       TESTIMONY OF WILLIAM D. YATES, CPA 
 
49 
 
For these reasons, residential customer complaints about DPAs often include the problem 
1 
that such customers were not able to obtain DPA terms based on a determination of their 
2 
individual financial circumstances as reflected by a completed FSF. A complete listing of 
3 
all KEDNY and KEDLI complaints coded by DPS as related to “Deferred Payment 
4 
Agreements”, with applicable citations to each complaint’s details in Exhibit ___(WDY-
5 
09) CONFIDENTIAL and Exhibit ___(WDY-10) CONFIDENTIAL can be found at 
6 
Exhibit ___(WDY-13), 28-29 and 30. 
  
7 
Q.  
Please provide an example of how KEDNY collections procedures do not ensure that 
8 
residential  customers  having difficulty  paying  their  bills  are  offered  affordable  
9 
DPAs meeting  the  statutory  requirements  set  forth  by  HEFPA. 
10 
A.   
KEDNY collection procedures require that customers who need DPAs with terms 
11 
addressing their individual financial circumstances apply in-person at walk-in customer 
12 
service centers. This non-HEFPA requirement unnecessarily impedes access to affordable 
13 
DPAs for customers unable to make such appearances for various reasons, including but 
14 
not limited to the fact that they: 
15 
• Are elderly, blind or disabled (EBD Customers); 
16 
• Cannot take time off from work, or; 
17 
• Cannot afford the expense of traveling to a customer service center. 
18 
Additionally, KEDNY’s “CRIS” information system is pre-configured to offer only two 
19 
types of DPAs to eligible customers: one that allows customers to voluntarily offer a down 
20 
payment of more than 15%, with the remaining balance paid over 10 months, and the 
21 
“standard offer” requiring a down payment of 15% of the balance, with the remaining 
22 
balance paid over a 10-month period; and another. (Exhibit ___(WDY-02) 
23 
CONFIDENTIAL at 204). 
24 
The Company’s collections procedures require that any customers whose individual 
25 
financial circumstances make them unable to afford either of the two alternatives described 
26 
above appear in-person at a customer service center to document their need for more 
27 
affordable DPA terms:

10-month period (KEDNY’s “standard” offer). (Exhibit ___(WDY-02) REDACTED at

2 
Q.  
Do you think it is reasonable for KEDNY to require eligible residential customers to 
3 
either appear in-person at a KEDNY customer service center in order to obtain a 
4 
DPA that addresses their individual financial circumstances; or otherwise be mailed 
5

8 
“…reasonable efforts to contact eligible customers or applicants by phone, mail 
9 
or in person for the purpose of offering a deferred payment agreement and 
10 
negotiating terms tailored to the customer’s financial circumstances, prior to 
11 
making the written offer of a deferred payment agreement…” 
12 
 (16 NYCRR § 11.10) 
13 
In situations called for by KEDNY’s collections procedures, the Company does not 
14 
negotiate “terms tailored to the customer’s financial circumstances, prior to making the 
15 
written offer of a deferred payment agreement” as required by HEFPA. Instead, it relies 
16 
upon pre-configured (default) DPA terms set by its CRIS system that can only be 
17 
overridden for customers who comply with the Company’s unreasonable requirement of 
18 
physically appearing at a service center so as to prove their need for more affordable terms.

12

16

17

18

19 
pay for gas and likely parking, if such is available. Without access to an automobile, taxi, 
20 
rideshare, or public transportation would be the only options.  In this regard, Exhibit 
21

a. Prior to converting from the legacy KEDLI customer service system 
1 
(known as CAS) to the current Customer Service System (known as CSS) 
2 
in 2014, KEDLI customers were required to complete Financial 
3 
Statements at one of KEDLI’s walk-in offices. The CSS system has a 
4 
built-in module that allows a customer service representative to enter 
5 
the customer-provided data points necessary to calculate eligibility. 
6 
Since the conversion to CSS, KEDLI customers can complete Financial 
7 
Statements over the telephone. KEDLI customers may still opt to fill out 
8 
the Financial Statement at a walk-in office. 
9 
Because KEDNY has not converted to CSS, the online Financial 
10 
Statement module is not available. KEDNY customers can complete a 
11 
Financial Statement at any of the walk-in customer service offices. A 
12 
customer may also submit a Financial Statement Form by fax when 
13 
working with a member of KEDNY’s PSC Complaints Team or a 
14

18 
The only basis cited by the Companies for the difference between KEDNY and 
19 
KEDLI in the required means by which residential customers must furnish evidence 
20 
of their need for more affordable DPAs is that “Because KEDNY has not converted 
21 
to CSS, the online Financial Statement module is not available”.  However, this 
22 
“constraint” should not be a constraint at all: KEDNY CSRs should be able to take 
23 
customer financial information over the phone and/or through the mail and enter 
24 
that information in the Company’s CRIS system themselves. It is simply not 
25 
necessary for customers to physically appear at a customer service center to provide 
26 
such evidence of their need, and it is an unreasonable burden to EBD customers to 
27 
require them to do so.

CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
 
       TESTIMONY OF WILLIAM D. YATES, CPA 
 
63 
 
• The requirement that customers physically appear at a service center so as to 
1 
provide evidence of their need for affordable DPA terms places an undue burden 
2 
on all customers, and a disparate burden on elderly, blind and/or disabled 
3 
customers; customers with serious medical conditions; and low income customers. 
4 
According to the testimony of the Company’s Shared Services Panel, there are only 
5 
four walk-in customer services centers in KEDNY’s service area where customers 
6 
can make payments or seek assistance with service issues. There are two offices in 
7 
Brooklyn (1535 Pitkin Avenue and 345 Jay Street), one on Staten Island, and one 
8 
in Queens (8967 162nd Street). (Shared Services Panel Testimony at 56).  These 
9 
are the only locations in a service area with 1.2 million residential customers where 
10 
such customers may appear to fulfill the Company’s requirement to provide 
11 
evidence of their need for more affordable DPAs. Even able-bodied persons could 
12 
generally expect that a trip to one of KEDNY’s service centers during business 
13 
hours could consume a significant part of their weekday — time they may not have 
14 
because of work or other commitments. And by definition, customers who need 
15 
more affordable DPA terms cannot afford any costs of traveling to a customer 
16 
service center that would be likely for most such customers, whether by taxi, 
17 
rideshare or public transportation — given the limited number of service centers. 
18 
Since the Company’s requirement that customers appear in-person to provide 
19 
evidence of their need for more affordable DPAs is unnecessary, the burden placed 
20 
on such customers by the requirement is undue.       
21 
• Complaints filed by residential KEDNY customers with DPS provide evidence that 
22 
the Company’s requirement that customers required to appear in-person at 
23 
customer service centers often do not succeed in their effort to obtain affordable 
24 
DPAs even though they have complied with the Company’s unnecessary 
25 
requirement. 
26 
As is made evident from Complaint IDs 1174, 1540 and 1181 above — together 
27 
with other complaints for which citations are provided at (WDY-13), 2; customers 
28 
who fulfill the requirement to appear at a customer service center to provide support 
29 
1669


--- Page 342 ---
CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
 
       TESTIMONY OF WILLIAM D. YATES, CPA 
 
64 
 
for their need for more affordable DPA terms often do not succeed in their effort to 
1 
obtain such DPAs. This situation provides evidence that the Company’s 
2 
requirement that customers physically appear at a service center so as to provide 
3 
evidence of their need for more affordable DPA terms is frequently ineffective in 
4 
generating affordable DPAs, which — given the fact that the requirement is not 
5 
necessary and there are perfectly acceptable alternative means for providing 
6 
support for customers’ indication of financial need — is further evidence that the 
7 
requirement is unreasonable.  
8 
• The Company’s requirement that customers physically appear at a service center 
9 
so as to provide evidence of their need for more affordable DPA terms also runs 
10 
counter to the stated purpose of its proposal to implement “E-DPAs” as described 
11 
by the testimony of the Shared Services Panel. 
12 
According to the testimony of the Shared Services Panel, the stated purpose of 
13 
implementing E-DPAs would be to: 
14 
“… permit customers to enter a DPA via electronic means (“E-DPA”), 
15 
while continuing to offer the conventional methods for entering DPAs 
16 
already in place. Customers would be allowed to place a call to 
17 
National Grid, negotiate the terms of a DPA over the phone with a 
18 
representative, and subsequently have an electronic document prepared 
19 
for the customer’s review and electronic signature. The customer would 
20 
complete the DPA using electronic signature protocols authorized 
21 
under the Electronic Signature and Records Act, N.Y.S. Technology 
22 
Law, §§ 301-309 (“ESRA”).” 
23 
(Shared Services Panel at 57-58, Emphasis Added) 
24 
PULP supports that proposal, as it has with equivalent proposals in other rate cases. 
25 
However, KEDNY’s insistence upon its requirement that customers physically 
26 
appear at a service center so as to provide evidence of their need for more affordable 
27 
DPA terms undermines the rationale for moving toward E-DPAs. What purpose 
28 
would such a requirement serve? Surely it would be unreasonable and inequitable 
29 
to privilege one subset of customers – those with the technological resources and 
30 
1670


--- Page 343 ---
CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
 
       TESTIMONY OF WILLIAM D. YATES, CPA 
 
65 
 
savvy to sign E-DPAs – with the ability to complete such DPAs without having to 
1 
physically appear at a service center and provide evidence of their need for more 
2 
affordable DPA terms; while subjecting ratepayers lacking broadband and/or 
3 
computer proficiency to long and costly journeys to KEDNY customer service 
4 
centers to complete their DPAs to receive an affordable DPA – which is a core 
5 
requirement of HEFPA.  
6 
• Furthermore, the Company’s I/R responses, together with the resolution of 
7 
complaints by its residential customers to DPS, provide evidence that KEDNY 
8 
makes exceptions to its requirement that customers physically appear at a service 
9 
center so as to provide evidence of their need for more affordable DPA terms when 
10 
such customers complain to either the Company or DPS. 
11 
As also indicated by the Company’s response to I/R PULP-63, KEDNY makes 
12 
exceptions to its requirement that customers physically appear at a service center 
13 
so as to provide evidence of their need for more affordable DPA terms by 
14 
permitting them to “submit a Financial Statement Form by fax when working with 
15 
a member of KEDNY’s PSC Complaints Team or a Consumer Advocate”. (Exhibit 
16 
___(WDY-02) REDACTED at 755). The Company does not indicate what the basis 
17 
for such leniency is, thus it would not be unreasonable to conclude that the primary 
18 
reason is simply that a customer has complained. At best, this is added undue 
19 
burden for those who have the time and information resources to complain; but for 
20 
everyone else, it provides no opportunity for redress. Examples of exceptions 
21 
related to specific complaints by residential KEDNY customers include Complaint

IDs 1540 (Exhibit ___(WDY-09) REDACTED at 1,898), 2146 (Exhibit 
23 
___(WDY-09) REDACTED at 2,686) and 2470 (Exhibit ___(WDY-09) 
24 
REDACTED at 3,097). 
25 
The only conclusion that can be drawn from the evidence obtained is that the 
26 
apparent policy and noted instances of exceptions to KEDNY’s requirement that 
27 
customers physically appear at a service center so as to provide evidence of their 
28 
need for more affordable DPA terms is that exceptions are determined based on 
29 
1671

66 
 
whether customers complain.  This is not a rational basis for such a requirement 
1 
and provides more evidence that the requirement should be eliminated.  
2 
Proposals/Recommendations 
3 
KEDNY should abandon its requirement that residential customers physically must appear at 
4 
customer service centers so as to provide evidence of their need for more affordable DPA terms. 
5 
Q.  
How do you propose that this problem be resolved? 
6 
A.   
KEDNY should abandon its requirement that residential customers physically must appear 
7 
at customer service centers so as to provide evidence of their need for more affordable 
8 
DPA terms. Instead, the Company should modify its residential payment arrangement and 
9 
financial statement form (FSF) procedures to affirmatively instruct CSRs to negotiate 
10 
DPAs with residential customers that meet the statutory requirements of HEFPA. Specific 
11 
modifications should include: 
12 
• Instructing CSRs to negotiate such DPAs at the time residential customers first 
13 
communicate difficulty payment on their KEDNY bills, including providing the 
14 
KEDLI Residential Payment Terms Rights Disclosure Statement set to be 
15 
implemented by KEDLI by the end of 2019 (Exhibit __(WDY-02) REDACTED at 
16 
665); 
17 
• Instructing CSRs to negotiate DPAs in a manner tailored to the individual financial 
18 
circumstances of customers, in accordance with HEFPA; and 
19 
• Removing all restrictions currently imposed by the Company upon CSRs to solicit 
20 
and obtain FSFs from residential customers as a means of objectively determining 
21 
their individual financial circumstances and thereby serving as the basis for DPA 
22 
terms; 
23 
• Evidencing the agreement of terms between the Company and its customers 
24 
entering into DPAs with the signature of both parties. To the greatest extent 
25 
possible, this should be accomplished using the Company’s proposed E-DPA 
26 
1672


--- Page 345 ---
CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
 
       TESTIMONY OF WILLIAM D. YATES, CPA 
 
67 
 
capabilities. When not possible, the Company should require customers to return 
1 
signed copies of their agreements to evidence the agreement of the parties; and 
2 
• Reporting on DMM the monthly number of: 
3 
- 
Completed FSFs; 
4 
- 
Completed E-DPAs, and; 
5 
- 
Signed hardcopy DPAs completed, including the separate reporting of 
6 
Minimum DPAs, other more lenient DPAs, and Standard DPAs completed. 
7

Working Relationship between KEDNY and the New York City Human Resources 
1 
Administration (HRA); and KEDLI and the Nassau/Suffolk Departments of Social 
2 
Services (Nassau/Suffolk DSS).  
3 
Q. 
Please describe the working relationship between KEDNY and HRA; and KEDLI 
4 
and Nassau/Suffolk DSS, in regard to the companies’ residential customers. 
5 
A. 
There are four major areas requiring coordination between HRA/DSS and the Companies: 
6 
1. Social Services Law 131-s (“One-Shot”) Recipients; 
7 
2. Utility Guarantee/Direct Vendor Customers; 
8 
3. HEAP Recipients, and; 
9 
4. EAP Eligibility. 
10 
Generally, HRA/DSS will provide eligible public assistance (PA) customers having 
11 
difficulty paying their utility bills with grant or loan assistance that pays down up to four 
12 
months of utility arrears pursuant to 131-s of the Social Services Law. These grants or 
13 
loans (referred to colloquially as “one-shot deals”) pay only for charges for utility service; 
14 
not miscellaneous charges such as late payment charges; reconnect fees, Marshall's fees, 
15 
deposits, no-access fees, on-bill financing charges, etc.34 Depending on the customer’s 
16 
circumstances, 131-s assistance may result in guarantee payment of a PA customer’s future 
17 
KEDNY/KEDLI bills for up to six months (Utility Guarantee Customers, or UGC); or pay 
18 
their bills outright until HRA/DSS informs the Companies otherwise (Direct Vendor 
19 
Customers, or DVC). When the Companies receive confirmations of these actions from 
20 
HRA/DSS, they generally remove all arrears older than four months from the applicable 
21 
customers’ bills and holds them as “abeyances”. The “abeyance” amounts will be returned 
22 
to the customers’ bills when they are no longer being covered under the UGC or DVC 
23 
programs. During benefit periods, these customers incur no late payment charges on 
24 
amounts in “abeyance”; nor are they subject to other collections measures associated with 
25 
“abeyance” amounts, such as the issuance of final termination notices, actual terminations 
26 
of service, and credit agency reporting. Once PA benefit periods end, “abeyances” return 
27 
 
34 See, Office of Temporary and Disability Assistance, Temporary Assistance Energy Manual (2016) at 20, available 
at: https://otda.ny.gov/programs/temporary-assistance/TAEM.pdf. 
1674


--- Page 347 ---
CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
 
       TESTIMONY OF WILLIAM D. YATES, CPA 
 
69 
 
to customer bills and are subject to normal collections measures.35 (Exhibit ___(WDY-02) 
1 
REDACTED at 649-664). 
2 
Q.  
How are HEAP grants coordinated between KEDNY/KEDLI and HRA/DSS for the 
3 
benefit of the companies’ customers?  
4 
A. 
The Home Energy Assistance Program (HEAP) provides assistance to help income eligible 
5 
households pay energy bills. The program opens in November each year. HEAP grant 
6 
amounts vary and are based upon information provided by the applicant’s annual energy 
7 
costs and annual income. For 2018-19, renters with heat included in their rent can obtain 
8 
grants of up to $50.00. Households with utility-supplied gas or deliverable fuel are eligible 
9 
to receive between $350 and $585. Households facing utility shut off for non-payment can, 
10 
if eligible, receive an emergency grant of up to $275. (Exhibit ___(WDY-02) 
11 
CONFIDENTIAL at 355). 
12 
KEDLI has an agreement with the PSC whereby notification of a HEAP benefit will either 
13 
reinstate a DPA or result in a new minimum payment agreement being negotiated once all 
14 
HEAP benefits have been exhausted. In order for customers to qualify, they must call 
15 
within thirty (30) days from the date the last HEAP pledge was entered on the account. 
16 
(Exhibit ___(WDY-02) CONFIDENTIAL at 20). KEDNY has a similar policy, whereby 
17 
the Company will consider any regular and/or emergency HEAP payment as entitling the 
18 
customer to an affordable and reasonable deferred payment agreement, regardless of any 
19 
previous DPA defaults. KEDNY will reinstate a defaulted payment agreement when the 
20 
grant is sufficient to cover the missed payments. If the grant amount is not sufficient for 
21 
reinstatement, the Company will activate a new deferred payment agreement. The 
22 
remaining balance is then rolled into a new deferred payment agreement with the same 
23 
terms and an extension of the installment period. (Exhibit ___(WDY-02) 
24 
CONFIDENTIAL at 355). 
25 
Both KEDNY and KEDLI waive the requirement for customers to complete an FSF in 
26 
order to obtain a Minimum DPA if they were promised an Emergency HEAP grant within 
27 
 
35 SSL § 131-s(6) 
1675

CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310

the past thirty (30) days, as long as such customers return a signed copy of the DPA contract 
1 
by the due date. (Exhibit ___(WDY-02) CONFIDENTIAL at 69, KEDNY 489). 
2 
KEDNY’s collections procedures indicate that many customers are given payment 
3 
agreements which make them ineligible for the Emergency HEAP grant. Therefore, CSRs 
4 
are cautioned that before setting up a DPA they should check to see if a customer meets 
5 
the HEAP income eligibility guidelines and, if so, and they have a shut off notice, to refer 
6 
them to HRA. (Exhibit ___(WDY-02) CONFIDENTIAL at 637).

Q.  
Are there ever any problems in the operation of One-Shot grants, UG/DV 
8 
promises/payments, and HEAP grants between KEDNY/KEDLI and HRA/DSS that 
9 
affect the Companies’ customers negatively? 
10 
A. 
Yes. In my review of KEDNY and KEDLI residential complaints to DPS, I found problems 
11 
with the fulfillment of HRA/DSS transactions to the Companies (for the benefit of their 
12 
customers) falling into several categories, including but not limited to: 
13 
• HRA/DSS Case Closings 
14 
When HRA/DSS stop guaranteeing/paying UGC/DVC customer bills, the 
15 
respective agencies notify the Companies, which then hold the affected customers 
16 
responsible for their own bills and return any balances that had been held in 
17 
abeyance to their accounts. Often, this transition comes as a big surprise to the 
18 
affected customers. (Indeed, abeyances can remain off customer bills for years; in 
19 
some cases well beyond what should be collectible pursuant to applicable statutes 
20 
of limitations and Commission practice.) 
21 
• Promised Payments Not Made, or Payments Made Then Rescinded by HRA/DSS 
22 
KEDNY and KEDLI hold One-Shot, UGC/DVC, and HEAP customers responsible 
23 
for HRA/DSS payments promised but not made; and/or HRA/DSS payments made 
24 
but then rescinded. “Abeyance” amounts associated with One-Shot grants and 
25 
UGC/DVC periods then return to the customer’s bill on top of the deficient 
26 
HRA/DSS payments, frequently with little or no explanation for such charges. 
27 
1676


--- Page 349 ---
CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
 
       TESTIMONY OF WILLIAM D. YATES, CPA 
 
71 
 
• 131-s and/or HEAP Funds not Received by Expiration of Final Termination 
1 
Notices 
2 
Residential customers of the Companies whose applications for One-Shot and/or 
3 
HEAP assistance through HRA/DSS take longer to process and be paid to their 
4 
KEDNY/KEDLI accounts than the time available before final termination notice 
5 
(FTN) expiration are at risk of service termination. Customers who wind up 
6 
complaining about this situation to DPS often indicate they had applied for 
7 
assistance before filing their complaints. In many cases, their complaints simply 
8 
request additional time beyond the initial extensions provided by the Companies to 
9 
allow assistance for which they had already applied, and in some cases been 
10 
approved, to be remitted to KEDNY or KEDLI. 
11 
In my review of residential customer complaints to DPS, I noted at least 108 KEDNY 
12 
complaints and 5 KEDLI complaints dealing with promised but unreceived HRA/DSS 
13 
assistance, which had a negative impact on the Companies’ customers. Exhibit ___(WDY-
14 
13) at 12 and 18 provide a list of these complaints, with applicable citations to the actual 
15 
complaint details in Exhibit ___(WDY-9) CONFIDENTIAL and Exhibit ___(WDY-10) 
16 
CONFIDENTIAL.

Proposals/Recommendations 
18 
The Companies should prepare customers who will experience the return of abeyance amounts to 
19 
their monthly bill and/or who have discrepancies between their KEDNY/KEDLI and HRA/DSS 
20 
records. Six years of detailed billing data should be made available to e-Billing to residential 
21 
customers as soon as they enroll in e-Billing. 
22 
Q.  
What is your proposal to deal with these problems? 
23 
A. 
Much more needs to be done to prepare customers who will experience the return of 
24 
abeyance amounts to their monthly bill and/or have discrepancies between their 
25 
KEDNY/KEDLI and HRA/DSS records. My recommendations are for the Companies to: 
26 
1677

• Provide access to six years of detailed billing data through the Company’s e-Billing 
1

2 
enroll in e-Billing, thus reducing the confusion and disputes that might otherwise 
3

4

11

12 
billing customers) and through the mail anytime there is an update/change for non-
13

Medical Emergency, Life Support Equipment; & Elderly, Blind or Disability Customers 
12 
Q.  
What did you find in your review of the Companies’ collections practices with regards 
13 
to elderly, blind or disability (EBD) customers? 
14 
A. 
In the KEDNY/KEDLI service areas, demographic trends reflected by the U.S. Census 
15 
Bureau’s American Community Survey (ACS) from 2010 through 2017 show a steady 
16 
increase in householders age 65 or older and persons with disabilities. Charts 8 and 9 reflect 
17 
these trends: 
18 
 
19 
1680


--- Page 353 ---
CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310 
 
       TESTIMONY OF WILLIAM D. YATES, CPA 
 
75 
 
Exhibit ___(WDY-04) at 17 
1 
As can be seen in Charts 8 - 9, the number of householders age 65 and older in the KEDNY 
2 
and KEDLI service areas increased 17% between 2010 and 2017, to 430,431 and 264,230 
3 
households, respectively. The number of persons with disabilities in the KEDNY service 
4 
area rose 4%, to 247,502; while the number of persons with disabilities in the KEDLI 
5 
service area rose 1%, to 111,903 from 2012 to 2017. 
6 
Special procedures are required by HEFPA before a utility may terminate service to a 
7 
residential customer whose household is comprised of persons who are all either elderly, 
8 
blind or disabled.36 For KEDNY/KEDLI customers, the most important issue faced by 
9 
customers with EBD household members is understanding the HEFPA requirements for 
10 
such protections and getting their accounts coded accordingly. Given the trends identified 
11 
in Charts 8-9, it is therefore not surprising that 415 KEDNY customers and 108 KEDLI 
12 
customers indicated in their complaints from 2016 to 2018 that at least one member of the 
13 
customer’s household is elderly, blind or disabled. (Exhibit ___(WDY-13) at 5-8, 14-15) 
14 
The Companies’ procedures for coding residential customers accounts for “protected” 
15 
under HEFPA can be found at Exhibit ___(WDY-02) REDACTED, 670 - 679. 
16

22 
customers with serious medical conditions was understanding the requirements HEFPA set 
23 
forth in order to protect their accounts from service termination and getting their accounts 
24 
coded accordingly.37 DPS representatives either coded complaints as “Medical 
25 
Emergency” and/or provided education and assistance to these customers about the 
26 
provisions for special protections under HEFPA regarding the termination, disconnection

Balance Transfers 
  
4 
Q.  
What did you find in your review of the Companies’ collections practices with regards 
5 
to residential customer balance transfers? 
6 
A. 
The Companies’ procedures were provided in response to I/R PULP-25 and can be found 
7 
at Exhibit ___(WDY-02) REDACTED, 734. These procedures require that KEDNY and 
8 
KEDLI CSRs refrain from transferring amounts owed to active customer accounts in 
9 
situations where their closed (“finaled”) accounts are past the applicable statute of 
10 
limitations (six years). Despite this instruction, however, three KEDNY residential 
11 
complaints to DPS from 2013 through 2018, provided by the Companies in response to I/R 
12 
PULP-26, were found to involve transfers of balances older than six years and therefore 
13 
the resolution of these complaints required reversing the transfers. In addition, six other 
14 
incorrect KEDNY transfers had to be reversed in resolution of customer complaints. 
15 
(Exhibit ___(WDY-02) REDACTED at 747 -750). 
16 
A complete listing of complaints filed by residential KEDNY/KEDNY customers 
17 
involving balance transfers from 2016 through 2018 (obtained by PULP through FOIL), 
18 
with applicable citations to each complaint’s details in Exhibits ___(WDY-09) 
19 
CONFIDENTIAL and Exhibits ___(WDY-10) CONFIDENTIAL, can be found at Exhibit 
20 
___(WDY-13), 13 and 19. 
21 
Proposals/Recommendations 
22 
The Companies should engage in outreach to customers whose accounts will be subject to balance 
23 
transfers in advance of such actions, and provide affected customers with an opportunity for 
24 
dispute resolution  
25 
Q.  
What is your recommendation for preventing incorrect balance transfers to the 
26 
accounts of residential KEDNY and KEDLI customers?  
27 
1683


--- Page 356 ---
CASES 19-G-0309 & 19-G-0310

78 
 
A. 
Prior to the recording of any balance transfers on residential customer accounts, the 
1 
Companies should engage in outreach to such customers to notify them of the Companies’ 
2 
intention to make such transfers. Summaries of telephone discussions between CSRs and 
3 
customers on these calls should be transmitted to applicable customers, who should be 
4 
requested to return acknowledgements of their agreement with CSR summaries, and/or any 
5 
differences in their understandings of such phone conversations. 
6 
Additionally, automatic thirty-day extensions should be granted to residential customers 
7 
who have balances transferred to their accounts. Extensions should be commenced as of 
8 
the date any bill including such a transfer is mailed. During extension periods, affected 
9 
customers should be given the opportunity to engage with specially designated Company 
10 
personnel to dispute transfers customers contend are erroneous and to seek resolution of 
11 
such disputes. During the extension period, these customers should also be provided the 
12 
opportunity to complete an FSF and to negotiate a DPA with the Company that meets the 
13 
statutory requirements set forth under HEFPA; and all collections actions against related 
14 
to disputed amounts should be suspended.

Q.   Would it be punitive to establish

A.   I think that properly designed

using those -- those historical averages plus a 
significant allowance for outliers, which, in my 
testimony, is represented by a two-standard-deviation 
target, rather than simply a one-standard-deviation 
target. 
And on top of that, another way to 
have to deal with the -- the concern you're raising 
is to normalize the historical data that you're using 
so that if you have outliers within that historical 
data, you can disregard those in developing your 
historical average.

II. 
Scope of Testimony 
15 
Q. 
What is the purpose of your testimony in this proceeding? 
16 
A. 
MAC was engaged by The Brooklyn Union Gas Company d/b/a National Grid NY 
17 
(“KEDNY” or the “Company”) to conduct a depreciation analysis of its leak prone pipe 
18 
(“LPP”) plant assets.   The purpose of my testimony is to sponsor and describe the LPP 
19 
depreciation analysis conducted by MAC and present the necessary support for cost 
20 
recovery recommendations related to these assets.

Q. 
What is the purpose of the depreciation analysis you performed for KEDNY? 
1 
A. 
The purpose of the depreciation analysis is to derive appropriate accrual rates reflective 
2 
of engineering judgment and current projections for the next 20 years, relative to the LPP 
3 
assets under study.  Because the Company is proceeding with a program through which 
4 
these LPP assets will be replaced and retired on an accelerated schedule, this analysis is 
5 
intended to produce depreciation rates that will reflect an annual amortization and 
6 
recovery of these LPP assets along with their retirement costs, commonly called net 
7 
salvage, over the proposed amortization period. 
8 
 
9 
Q. 
What procedures did you employ in compiling your depreciation studies? 
10 
A. 
First, MAC created the depreciation study databases that utilized the Company’s vintage 
11 
survivors by LPP account as of December 31, 2018.  KEDNY also provided the cost of 
12 
removal, annual retirements, annual mileage of retirements, and annual number of 
13 
services history for the most recent seven years (2012-2018) to complete MAC’s analysis 
14 
of net salvage for the LPP accounts (See Exhibit ___ (PMN-4)). 
15 
 
16 
Q. 
Why have you chosen the seven-year period for your analysis of LPP? 
17 
A. 
The Company began its program of retiring/replacing LPP on an accelerated basis in 
18 
2015 and has ramped up its annual retirement miles for these specific assets to an 
19 
expected level going into the future.  Because the primary costs incurred in connection 
20 
with the program are labor-related, the most recent period was deemed to be most 
21 
representative of the costs that are likely to be incurred in connection with future 
22 
retirement efforts for these plant assets.  Exhibit ___ (PMN-4) sets forth the three-year 
23 
period of historical annual costs used to derive our proposed recommendations.  MAC 
24 
1713


--- Page 386 ---
Testimony of Paul M. Normand 
 
Page 7 of 9 
also examined data for the most recent four and seven year periods.  MAC’s conclusion 
1 
was that the three-year analysis was the most representative period.  It is important to 
2 
recognize that this analysis is only applicable to a portion of the few accounts associated 
3 
with LPP as identified in the exhibits.  Exhibit __ (PMN-5) sets forth the dollar amount 
4 
for each LPP-related account and the dollar portion related to LPP. 
5 
 
6 
Q. 
What approach did you use in developing your proposed accrual rates? 
7 
A. 
The depreciation analysis utilized the period over which the LPP replacement program 
8 
would be in effect to determine the period of time over which these assets would be 
9 
retired, their cost of removal would be incurred, and their full cost recovery would be 
10 
calculated and effectuated.  This depreciation approach is necessary to fully recover all 
11 
appropriate costs for these facilities within the retirement period proposed. 
12 
 
13 
Q. 
Did you prepare any actuarial analyses to assist in the life estimation process? 
14 
A. 
No, we did not analyze each account with respect to rolling bands (10 years) and 
15 
shrinking bands (one year) for each polynomial (first, second, and third degree) as this 
16 
was unnecessary because we are proposing a 20 year amortization of all LPP plant 
17 
accounts to align with the Company’s replacement program. 
18 
 
19

22 
policy, procedure, equipment, or practices, that might impact historical plant balances

and salvage, or retirement and removal costs associated with these depreciable assets.

18

19

20

21

22

1

2 
 
3 
Q. 
Do you believe it is reasonable for KEDNY to assess the increased depreciation rates 
4 
for its LPP plant accounts that you are recommending? 
5 
A. 
Yes.  If KEDNY does not assess the depreciation rates that MAC is recommending in 
6 
these proceedings, it will be unable to recover the costs of its LPP facilities from the 
7 
customers who have been served by those facilities.  Instead, KEDNY will be required to 
8 
recover the costs of those LPP facilities from future customers.  It is more equitable to 
9 
assess cost responsibility for the LPP facilities to customers that are currently benefitting 
10 
from the operation of those LPP facilities.

bands (one year) for each polynomial (first, second, and third degree) as this was 
15 
unnecessary because we are proposing a 20 year amortization of all LPP plant accounts to 
16 
align with the Company’s replacement program.

1 
Q. 
Do you believe it is reasonable for KEDLI to assess the increased depreciation rates 
2 
for its LPP plant accounts that you are recommending? 
3 
A. 
Yes.  If KEDLI does not assess the depreciation rates that MAC is recommending in these 
4 
proceedings, it will be unable to recover the full costs of its LPP facilities from the 
5 
customers who have been served by those facilities.  Instead, KEDLI will be required to 
6 
recover the costs of those LPP facilities from future customers.  It is more equitable to 
7 
assess cost responsibility for the LPP facilities to customers that are currently benefitting 
8 
from the operation of those LPP facilities.

5

8

13

19

A. 
Although Staff asserts (at 23) that it is “ultimately” concerned with the bill impacts of the 
21

22 
proposals are unreasonable in these cases.  It is my understanding that Staff is 
23 
1732


--- Page 405 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
Case 19-G-0310 
Rebuttal Testimony of Paul M. Normand 
 
Page 4 of 8 
 
recommending a decrease in base rates for KEDLI of $51.852 million annually and an 
1 
increase of only $2.205 million annually for KEDNY.  Given the size of the proposed 
2 
base rate increases in this case, even as proposed by KEDNY and KEDLI, it is not clear 
3 
why Staff believes that the impact of the Companies’ LPP depreciation proposals is 
4

5 
Companies’ LPP proposals in future rate cases, the Commission can always revisit the 
6 
Companies’ LPP depreciation rates in future cases if the Companies’ seek rate increases 
7 
that have bill impacts that the Commission determines to be unreasonable. 
8 
 
9 
Q. 
Staff asserts (at 23-24) that it is making the same proposals for KEDNY and KEDLI 
10 
for consistency.  Do you agree that there is need for consistent LPP depreciation 
11 
parameters? 
12 
A. 
No, I do not.  Depreciation rates are typically set on the basis of individual utilities’ facts 
13 
and circumstances.  There is no reason why KEDNY and KEDLI should be required to 
14 
amortize LPP costs in consistent fashion.  In this instance, geography alone dictates that a 
15 
difference can be expected because of the urban nature of KEDNY’s service territory and 
16 
the more suburban/rural nature of KEDLI’s territory. 
17 
 
18 
Q. 
Staff claims (at 24) that it is equitable for current and future customers to pay the 
19 
costs of the replacement of LPP because it is being done for safety and reliability 
20 
reasons.  Do you agree? 
21 
A. 
No.  A significant amount of the facilities that a gas distribution system replaces is 
22 
replaced due to safety and reliability concerns.  The existence of such concerns does not 
23 
1733


--- Page 406 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
Case 19-G-0310 
Rebuttal Testimony of Paul M. Normand 
 
Page 5 of 8 
 
make it more equitable for the Commission to require future customers to pay for both 
1 
facilities that are replaced as well as the new facilities that replace them.  The Companies 
2 
and Staff agree that the Companies’ aging LPP infrastructure should be replaced over 20 
3 
years.  The recovery of the costs of the replaced facilities should be in sync with the same 
4 
period of time.  This will result in an equitable cost recovery from all customers with 
5 
minimal cost shifting from one population of customers to another (future) population.  
6 
This is particularly important because the cost of removing LPP facilities, which is 
7 
primarily labor expense, will rise over the period in which the LPP is replaced and the 
8 
Companies’ are using current average historical costs to estimate the cost removal in 
9 
future years.  For this reason alone, using the current proposed cost of removal is 
10 
understated and future customers will likely be required to pay more.  In addition, it is 
11 
more important than ever for the Companies to secure an equitable level of LPP cost 
12 
recovery from current customers because there is a likelihood that the Companies’ 
13 
customer counts may drop in the future as a result of political decisions to reduce the use 
14 
of fossil fuels.  Passing cost responsibility for the cost of removing LPP assets that are 
15 
being replaced today onto a diminishing number of future customers would be 
16 
particularly unfair.  Staff’s recommendation to extend responsibility for the cost of LPP 
17 
assets for a period of 10 years beyond the time in which those assets will be replaced 
18 
does not result in a fair and equitable assignment of costs. 
19 
 
20 
Q. 
Staff asserts (at 24-25) that the use of a four-year historical analysis of net salvage is 
21 
more reasonable than the Companies’ proposed three-year analysis.  Do you agree? 
22 
1734


--- Page 407 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
Case 19-G-0310 
Rebuttal Testimony of Paul M. Normand 
 
Page 6 of 8 
 
A. 
No.  Staff’s proposal looks further back in time to reflect a lower cost of removal.  
1 
However, there is no reason to believe that the cost of removal is likely to decrease in the 
2 
future.  To the contrary, this cost is more likely to increase because the cost is driven 
3 
primarily by labor costs that will continue to increase over time.  This is driven by both 
4 
the impact of general inflation and what is likely to be a tightened market for the skilled 
5 
labor needed to replace utility facilities.  Staff’s use of data from 2015 to reduce net 
6 
salvage costs makes it more likely that the Company will underrecover its net salvage 
7 
costs.   
8 
 
 
9 
 
The analysis presented in my direct testimony focused on looking at a span of years that 
10 
would capture annual fluctuations in reported cost of removal data as well as the most 
11 
recent data to capture as fully as possible the costs of the ongoing replacement programs.  
12 
The 2015 cost of removal data that Staff seeks to include was omitted because costs in 
13 
the first year of a 20-year replacement program are less likely to be representative of the 
14 
ongoing costs of a mature program. 
15 
 
 
16 
 
As with all these types of projects, time is required to ramp up the annual activity of a 20-
17 
year program.  My review started with the KEDNY data as shown on Exhibit __ (PMN-
18 
4), pages 3 and 4 that presents annual costs relating to retirements and cost to retire.  In 
19 
reviewing this data, I observed that the annual fluctuations were considerable, which is 
20 
not unusual because, in many instances, there is a lag in the reporting of data for different 
21 
years.  As a result of this data lag, I suggested that a span of several years would be 
22 
appropriate.  I chose three years as the most representative over four years as 
23 
1735


--- Page 408 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
Case 19-G-0310 
Rebuttal Testimony of Paul M. Normand 
 
Page 7 of 8 
 
recommended by Staff because adding the fourth year showed some high potential dollar 
1 
lags that materially affected the results.  Given the fact that the cost of replacing LPP is 
2 
likely to rise over time, I determined that 2015 reported data should not be included in the 
3 
analysis because it would reduce the cost based on a set of start-up circumstances that 
4 
were unlikely to recur.  Adding 2015 data to the analysis simply dilutes the cost 
5 
estimates. 
6 
 
 
7 
 
To validate the reasonableness of my approach, I prepared an update to the analysis that I 
8 
originally included as Exhibit __ (PMN-4).  That update is included as Exhibit __ (PMN-
9 
2R) and reflects the addition of actual retirement data for the first eight months (January 
10 
– August) of calendar year 2019 and annualizes that data for the full 12 months.  Data is 
11 
provided for KEDNY on page 1 and KEDLI on page 2 and the annualization of the data 
12 
is set forth on page 3.  A comparison of the 2019 data to the earlier years clearly shows 
13 
that the proposed three-year average is the most appropriate data to use to estimate the 
14 
cost of removal going forward and that the 2015 data is an outlier. 
15 
 
16 
IV. 
Response to NYC 
17 
Q. 
What is your understanding of Mr. Kollen’s recommendation concerning the 
18 
Companies’ LPP depreciation proposals? 
19 
A. 
Mr. Kollen asserts that the Companies’ proposals to accelerate the recovery of LPP assets 
20 
should be rejected.  He further asserts that the Companies should be required to defer 
21 
their actual costs of removal for future recovery and then recover the actual costs through 
22 
existing depreciation rates. 
23 
1736


--- Page 409 ---
Case 19-G-0309 
Case 19-G-0310 
Rebuttal Testimony of Paul M. Normand 
 
Page 8 of 8 
 
Q. 
Do you agree with Mr. Kollen’s recommendations? 
1 
A. 
No.  Mr. Kollen’s recommendations are nothing more than an exercise in kicking the can 
2 
down the road so that future customers will be required to bear significant portions of the 
3 
costs of LPP assets that are being retired and the costs of removing those facilities as well 
4 
as the costs of the replacement facilities.  His proposal would negatively affect the 
5 
Companies’ cash flow and is completely unfair to future customers. 
6 
 
7 
Q. 
Do you agree with Mr. Kollen’s concerns about the reliability of your estimates of 
8 
cost of removal? 
9 
A. 
No, I do not.  Using a multi-year average of costs of removal mitigates concerns about 
10 
the reliability of the estimates.  Moreover, the increased cost of labor that is likely to be 
11 
incurred in the future, which is the bulk of cost to be recovered, is not captured by the 
12 
analysis.  Thus, there is little risk that the cost of removal that the rates will recover is 
13 
likely to be less than the actual cost.
